Heegaard Floer homology and plane curves with non-cuspidal singularities
Maciej Borodzik, Beibei Liu, Ian Zemke

Abstract
. We study possible configurations of singular points occuring on general algebraic curves in ℂ ​ P 2 \mathbb{C}P^{2} via Floer theory. To achieve this, we describe a general formula for the H 1 H_{1} -action on the knot Floer complex of the knotification of a link in S 3 S^{3} , in terms of natural actions on the link Floer complex of the original link. This result may be of interest on its own.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
primary: 14H50, secondary: 57K18, 14B05, 57R58

1.
Introduction

1.1.
General context

Let
C
be a complex curve in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
. The curve
C
is called
rational
, if
C
is irreducible and there exists a continuous degree one map from
S^{2}

to
C
. The curve
C
is called
cuspidal
, if all its singularities have one branch (i.e. their links have one component). The problem of classifying rational cuspidal curves was posed in the 19th century and has not been fully resolved ever since, despite significant recent progress. For instance, Palka and Pełka gave a partial classification (in fact, a full classification under a so-called rigidity conjecture)

[
45
,
46
]
, and Koras and Palka
[
44
,
26
]
solved a century old conjecture of Coolidge–Nagata
[
8
,
33
]
related to cuspidal curves.

In
[
13
]
, Fernandez de Bobadilla, Luengo, Melle-Hernandez and Némethi indicated a connection between Seiberg–Witten invariants and rational cuspidal curves. As a consequence of these connections, they stated a conjecture binding coefficients
of Alexander polynomials of singular points of a rational cuspidal curve. This conjecture was proved in
[
5
]
; we refer to
[
2
]
for a precise connection of the results of
[
5
]
and the conjecture of
[
13
]
. The methods of
[
5
]
used the relation
of semigroups of singular points with
V_{s}
-invariants of knots together with the Ozsváth–Szabó
d
-invariant inequality.

The methods of
[
5
]
were later generalized by Bodnár, Borodzik, Celoria, Golla, Hedden and Livingston
[
1
,
4
]
to the case
of non-rational cuspidal curves. Further generalization required better understanding of Heegaard Floer theory of multi-component links of
singularities. The techniques used in the present paper build on recent developments in the Heegaard Floer TQFT due to the third author as well as many others; see
[
20
,
22
,
51
,
52
,
55
,
54
]
.

1.2.
Overview of the paper

Given a
k
-component link
L\subset S^{3}
we
use the aforementioned Heegaard Floer TQFT to recover the knot Floer complex of the knotification
\widehat{L}
of
L
together with the action of
\Lambda^{*}H_{1}(\#^{k-1}S^{2}\times S^{1})
on it. This is a generalization of the result of Ozsváth and Szabó, who established
an isomorphism between the hat version of link Floer homology of the link and of its knotification
[
42
]
. We prove a particularly useful technical result (Proposition
2.10
) which allows us to compute the action of
\Lambda^{*}H_{1}(\#^{k-1}S^{2}\times S^{1})
on the knot Floer homology of a knotification in terms of a link diagram for
L
.

Using this general result, we compute the knot Floer complexes of the knotifications of the

T(2,2n)
torus link and of its mirror, as well as the action of
H_{1}(S^{2}\times S^{1})
. In particular, we are able to compute
the invariants
V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}
and
V^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}
of these knots. To the best of our knowledge, these computations have not appeared in the literature before.

Our main focus is on general curves in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
. To generalize results of
[
1
,
4
]
to the setting of complex curves
C\subset\mathbb{C}P^{2}
with non-cuspidal singularities, we take
a precisely defined ‘tubular’ neighborhood
N
of
C
. The boundary
Y=\partial N
can be described as a surgery on a link
L

in
\#^{\rho}S^{2}\times S^{1}
, where
L
is a suitable connected sum of knotifications of links of singularities and Borromean knots, and
\rho
can be expressed
in terms of topology of
C
. As in
[
1
,
4
]
, the manifold
Y
bounds a four-manifold
X=\mathbb{C}P^{2}\setminus N
, with
trivial intersection form. Using Ozsváth–Szabó’s
d
-invariant inequality in the version proved by Levine and Ruberman
[
29
]
,
we obtain restrictions on
V^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}(L)
and
V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}(L)
.

While we are able to provide statements even for reducible curves
C
, in the concrete applications we focus on irreducible curves. The first
case we deal with is when
C
has some finite number of cuspidal singularities as well as singularities whose links are
T(2,2n)
torus links.
We obtain the following result.

Theorem

(see Theorem
6.4
)
.

Let
C
be a reduced curve of degree
d
and genus
g
.
Suppose that
C
has cuspidal singular points
z_{1},\dots,z_{\nu}
, whose semigroup counting functions are
R_{1},\dots,R_{\nu}
, respectively.
Assume that apart from these
\nu
points, the curve
C
has, for each
n\geq 1
,
m_{n}\geq 0
singular points whose links are
T(2,2n)
and no other singularities.
Define

\eta_{+}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}m_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa_{+}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}nm_{n}.

For any
k=1,\dots,d-2
, we have:

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq j\leq g}\min_{0\leq i\leq\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}}\left(R(kd+1-\eta_{+}-2i-2j)+i+j\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq j\leq g+\kappa_{+}}\left(R(kd+1-2\gamma)+j\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}.

Here
R
denotes the infimal convolution of the functions
R_{1},\dots,R_{\nu}
.

We also study curves with negative double points, and more generally curves with singularities whose links are
-T(2,2n)
torus links. While a complex curve cannot have such singular points, we give a precise definition of a smooth curve with more general singularities, see Section
1.5
. There is another technical issue: the
V_{s}
invariants of tensor products of positive staircase complexes (like the knot Floer complex of an L-space knot) can be easily computed. Tensoring
a negative staircase complex (like the knot Floer complex of the mirror of an L-space knot) and several positive staircase complexes
yields a chain complex whose

V_{s}
invariants cannot, in general, be expressed in terms of the
V_{s}
invariants of the initial complexes, not unless there is precisely one
positive staircase complex involved. An extra assumption in the next theorem reflects this problem.

Theorem

(Theorem
6.18
)
.

Suppose
C
is a genus
g
degree
d

singular curve in the smooth category as in Definition
1.3
with a cuspidal singular point
z
,

\underline{m}_{n}
singularities whose links are
-T(2,2n)
, and no other singular points. Suppose further that the
genus of
C
is given by (
6.1
).

Then, for any
k=1,\dots,d-2
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq j\leq g+\kappa_{-}}\left(R(kd+1-2j)+j\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g+k_{-},

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq i\leq g}\max_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-}}\left(R(kd+1-2i-2j-\eta_{-})+i+j\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+k_{-}-\eta_{-},

where
R
is the semigroup counting function for the singular point
z
, and
\eta_{-}=\sum\underline{m}_{n},\kappa_{-}=\sum\underline{m}_{n}n
.

It is possible (and actually evident from their proofs)
to mix results of Theorem
6.4
and
6.18
, by considering curves with both positive
and negative
T(2,2n)
torus link singularities. The calculations are only a bit harder, but we were not able to find applications that would justify
stating this more general result.

1.3.
Applications and perspectives

Apart from an interest of its own of Theorems
6.4
and
6.18
, we provide some specific applications.
First of all, there is a growing interest in the question of “trading genus for double points”. To be more precise, given a surface of genus
g
, one can ask whether it is possible to deform it to a genus
g-1
surface with an extra positive or negative double point. In the context of the surfaces in a four-ball with
fixed boundaries, this question is related to studying the difference between the clasp number and the smooth four-ball genus;
see
[
9
,
12
,
23
,
27
,
37
]
. We deal with a variation of this question, which concerns trading
genus of a closed surface in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
for double points, while preserving the remaining singularities. Given that examples of higher genus (and higher degree) curves in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}

do not appear often in the literature, it is much harder to provide concrete cases, where our obstructions can be applied.
We provide the following two concrete examples.

Example 1.1

(see Example
6.22
)
.

Let
C_{n}
be a genus one Orevkov curve of Example
6.16
. The genus cannot be traded for a negative double point.
It is unknown if the genus can be traded for a positive double point (methods of the present paper are insufficient to obstruct this).

Example 1.2

(see Example
6.15
)
.

If there exist genus one curves of degree 27 (resp. 33) with a single singularity whose link is a
T(10,73)
torus knot (resp.
T(12,91)
torus
knot) (items (f) and (g) in
[
4
, Theorem 9.1]
), then the genus cannot be traded for a positive double point.

We note that the potential curves of Example
1.2
pass the obstruction of
[
4
]
, but at present we do not have a way to construct them explicitly, or to obstruct their existence by other means.

Our last application concerns complex algebraic curves all of whose singular points are of type
A_{m}
, that is, singular points whose links are

T(2,m+1)
torus knots/links. Such curves are extensively studied in the literature; see for instance the survey
[
30
]
.
We give a bound for the maximal
n
such that an algebraic curve of the degree
d
has an
A_{2n}
singularity, as well as a bound on the
number of cusps of an algebraic curve of degree
d
with nodes and ordinary cusps, see Section
7
. While these bounds are not the best found in the literature, they are the first bounds using Floer theories. There is a chance that these bounds could be refined by using other versions of Floer homology.

As a perspective and a possibility for future research we indicate that the methods can be used to study line arrangements in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
. The only
missing ingredient is the computation of Heegaard Floer chain complex of a
T(d,d)
torus link for
d>2
, and the
H_{1}
action on the knotification.

1.4.
Organization

In Subsection
1.5
we recall the notion of a smooth surface with singularities. As we never use any complex or even symplectic structures on the surfaces, this notion seems to be the most suitable for our applications. A reader interested in algebraic geometry can safely
consider all curves as complex ones; the only exception will be Theorem
6.18
.

Section
2
reviews Heegaard Floer theory. After recalling variuous known definitions and results, we show how to
obtain the knot Floer chain complex of the knotification of links, as well the
H_{1}/\Tors
action. Later, we recall the Levine–Ruberman versions of
d
-invariants and recall
definitions of
V_{s}
invariants.

Section
3
constructs a tubular neighborhood
N
of a singular curve and presents the boundary
Y
of this neighborhood as a surgery on a link
L
in
\#^{\rho}S^{2}\times S^{1}
for a concrete value of
\rho
. We then compute homological invariants of
Y
,
N
and
\mathbb{C}P^{2}\setminus N
. In particular,
we study which Spin
c
structures on
Y
extend over
\mathbb{C}P^{2}\setminus N
. These computations are slight generalizations of calculations
of
[
1
,
4
,
5
]
.

In Section
4
we introduce several links which play prominent roles in our paper, and are used to build the links from Section
3
. For our
applications, these are algebraic knots, or, more generally, L-space knots; see Subsection
4.1
, as well as
knotifications of
T(2,2n)
torus links and their mirrors. The detailed
construction for the Hopf link is presented in Subsection
4.2
, and the generalization to knotifications of arbitrary
T(2,2n)
torus link is given in Subsection
4.3
. We conclude Section
4

with Subsection
4.4
, where we recall the computations of the Heegaard Floer chain complex of the Borromean knot
{\mathcal{B}_{0}}
.

Section
5
contains some important computations that happen behind a scene. We show how to recover the
V_{s}
invariant
of a product of positive and negative staircases. A precise statement is given in Proposition
5.8
.
We show that the assumptions in the second item of that proposition is necessary in Subsection
5.3
.

Next, we consider tensor products of knots in manifolds with
b_{1}>0
. It turns out that most of the
knots that we encounter share a property, which
greatly facilitates our computations, namely they have
split towers
, see Definition
5.19
.

Section
6
contains the proofs of Theorems
6.4
and
6.18
. The main technical result
is Proposition
6.3
, which computes the
d
-invariants of
Y
in terms of the semigroup counting functions
of knots of cuspidal singularities. We also compare Theorems
6.4
and
6.18
with bounds
for cuspidal curves of higher genus in Subsections
6.3
and
6.5
.

In Section
7
we consider curves whose only singularities are
T(2,k)
torus knots or links. The main results are Theorem
7.2
, and Theorem
7.4
. Theorem
7.2
concerns curves which have only double points and ordinary cusps. Theorem
7.4
bounds the maximal index
n
such that a curve of degree
d
can have an
A_{2n}
singularity. The main algebraic tool is Proposition
7.6
, which allows us to turn the obstructions from the previous sections into a closed formula.

1.5.
Singular curves in the smooth category

The methods we use in the present article work in a smooth category. A term “smooth surface with singularities” might be misleading,
therefore we make precise our terminology.

Definition 1.3
.

A
singular curve in the smooth category
in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
is a smooth map
\iota\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}P^{2}
, which is a diffeomorphism onto its image except for finitely many points.
The surface
\Sigma
is a smooth, closed, two-dimensional manifold.
The image
C=\iota(\Sigma)
is a smooth submanifold except possibly at points
z_{1},\dots,z_{N}
. For each such point
z_{i}
, there exists a ball
B_{i}\subset\mathbb{C}P^{2}
with center
z_{i}
, such that
C
is transverse to
\partial B_{i}
and
C\cap B_{i}
is a cone over
K_{i}=\partial B_{i}\cap C
.

The
genus
of a singular curve is the genus of
\Sigma
. The set
K_{i}
is a link in
\partial B_{i}
. It is called a
link of singularity
.

Algebraic curves have one more restriction. The link of a singularity of an algebraic curve is an algebraic link (see
[
10
]
).
For example, every algebraic knot is a sufficiently positive iterated torus knot.

Definition 1.4
.

A singular curve in the smooth category is of
algebraic type
if all links of its singularities are algebraic links.
A singular curve in the smooth category is of
weakly algebraic type
if all links of its singularities are either algebraic links
or their mirrors.

In the present paper, unless specified otherwise, one can always replace a term “algebraic curve” by a “singular curve in the smooth category of weakly algebraic type”. We refer to
[
14
]
for a related construction.

.

for spotting a mistake in the first version of the paper.

The first named author was supported by OPUS 2019/B/35/ST1/01120 grant
of the Polish National Science. The second named author is grateful to the Max Planck Institute for Mathematics in Bonn for its hospitality and financial support, where the project began.

2.
Review of Heegaard Floer theory

2.1.
Heegaard Floer complexes with multiple basepoints

Definition 2.1
.

A
multi-pointed Heegaard diagram
for a 3-manifold
Y

is a quadruple
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathbf{w})
where:

•

\Sigma
is a genus
g
surface, which splits
Y
into two genus
g
handlebodies,
U_{\alpha}
and
U_{\beta}
, and
\mathbf{w}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})
is a nonempty set of basepoints in
\Sigma
.

•

\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g+n-1})
and
\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{g+n-1})

are collections of simple closed curves on
\Sigma
, where
n=|\mathbf{w}|
. Each curve in
\bm{\alpha}
bounds a compressing disk in
U_{\alpha}
, and each curve in
\bm{\beta}
bounds a compressing disk in
U_{\beta}
. Furthermore, the curves in
\bm{\alpha}
are pairwise disjoint, and similarly for
\bm{\beta}
.

•

The curves
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\beta}
are transverse.

•

The curves in
\bm{\alpha}
are linearly independent in
H_{1}(\Sigma\setminus\bm{\mathbf{w}})
, and similarly for
\bm{\beta}
.

Ozsváth and Szabó
[
39
, Section 2.6]
describe a map

\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{w}}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}\to\Spin^{c}(Y).

Given a Heegaard diagram of
Y
, we define a Floer chain complex

\CF^{-}(Y,\mathbf{w},\mathfrak{s})
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U_{1},\dots,U_{n}]
.
The chain complex is generated over
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U_{1},\dots,U_{n}]
by intersection points in
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
satisfying
\mathfrak{s}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\mathfrak{s}
. Here
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha},\mathbb{T}_{\beta}\subset\Sym^{g+n-1}(\Sigma)
are the two half-dimensional tori

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{g+n-1},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{T}_{\beta}=\beta_{1}\times\dots\times\beta_{g+n-1}.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
, the differential is defined by

(2.2)

\partial\mathbf{x}=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}\#(\mathcal{M}(\phi)/\mathbb{R})U_{1}^{n_{w_{1}}(\phi)}\cdots U_{n}^{n_{w_{n}(\phi)}}\mathbf{y}.

Here,
\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
denotes the set of homotopy classes of maps of a complex unit disk
\mathbb{D}
to
\Sym^{g+n-1}(\Sigma)
such that point
-i
is mapped to
\bm{\mathbf{x}}
, the point
i
is mapped to
\bm{\mathbf{y}}
,
\partial\mathbb{D}\cap\{\mathrm{Re}(z)<0\}
is mapped to
\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
and
\partial\mathbb{D}\cap\{\mathrm{Re}(z)>0\}
is mapped to
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
. The
space
\mathcal{M}(\phi)
is the moduli space of
J_{s}
-holomorphic disks representing
\phi
(for some 1-parameter family of almost complex structures
J_{s}
on
\Sym^{g+n-1}(\Sigma)
). The condition
that
\mu(\phi)=1
implies that
\mathcal{M}(\phi)/\mathbb{R}
is generically a finite set of points. The integers
n_{w_{i}}(\phi)
are intersection
numbers of
\{w_{i}\}\times\Sym^{g+n-2}(\Sigma)\subset\Sym^{g+n-1}(\Sigma)
with the image of
\phi
.

If
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
is torsion, then
\CF^{-}(Y,\mathbf{w},{{\mathfrak{s}}})
admits an absolute
\mathbb{Q}
-valued grading, which we denote by
\gr_{w}
. The differential decreases the grading by
1
. Multiplication
by any of the
U_{i}
decreases the grading by
-2
. Formally inverting the variables
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
in
\CF^{-}(Y,\mathbf{w},{{\mathfrak{s}}})
gives a chain complex
\CF^{\infty}(Y,\mathbf{w},{{\mathfrak{s}}})
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U_{1},U_{1}^{-1},\dots,U_{n},U_{n}^{-1}]
.

2.2.
The link Floer complex

For links in
S^{3}
, Ozsváth and Szabó
[
42
]
introduced the link Floer homology, which generalizes the knot Floer homology defined seperately by Rasmussen
[
47
]
and Ozsváth–Szabó
[
38
]
. We presently recall their construction.

Definition 2.3
.

An
oriented multi-pointed link

\mathbb{L}=(L,\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})
in
Y^{3}
is an oriented link
L
with two disjoint collections of basepoints
\mathbf{w}=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{z}=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\}
, such that as one traverses
L
, the basepoints alternate between
\mathbf{w}
and
\mathbf{z}
. Furthermore, each component of
L
has a positive (necessarily even) number of basepoints, and each component of
Y
contains at least one component of
L
.

Analogously to Definition
2.1
, we have the following:

Definition 2.4
.

A
multi-pointed Heegaard link diagram
for
\mathbb{L}=(L,\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})
in
Y^{3}
is a tuple
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})
satisfying the following:

•

(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathbf{w})
and
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathbf{z})
are embedded Heegaard diagrams for
(Y,\mathbf{w})
and
(Y,\mathbf{z})
, respectively, in the sense of Definition
2.1
.

•

L\cap\Sigma=\mathbf{w}\cup\mathbf{z}
, and furthermore
L
intersects
\Sigma
positively at
\mathbf{z}
and negatively at
\mathbf{w}
.

•

L\cap U_{\alpha}
(resp.
L\cap U_{\beta}
) is a boundary-parallel tangle in
U_{\alpha}
(resp.
U_{\beta}
).

Given a multi-pointed Heegaard link diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z})
for
(Y,\mathbb{L})
, the
link Floer chain complex
is defined as follows.
Let

\mathscr{R}^{-}=\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V}],\ \ \mathscr{R}^{\infty}=\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathscr{U},\mathscr{U}^{-1},\mathscr{V},\mathscr{V}^{-1}].

We define the chain complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\Sigma,\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\mathbf{w},\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{s})
to be the free
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-module generated by
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{w}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\mathfrak{s}
. The differential is given by the formula

(2.5)

\partial\mathbf{x}=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}\#(\mathcal{M}(\phi)/\mathbb{R})\mathscr{U}^{n_{w_{1}}(\phi)+\cdots+n_{w_{n}}(\phi)}\mathscr{V}^{n_{z_{1}}(\phi)+\cdots+n_{z_{n}}(\phi)}\cdot\mathbf{y},

extended
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-equivariantly. The differential
\partial
squares to 0.

There is a larger version of the link Floer complex, which we call the
full link Floer complex
, denoted by
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}_{\full}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
. As a module,
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}_{\full}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
is freely generated over the ring
\mathbb{F}[\mathscr{U}_{1},\dots\mathscr{U}_{n},\mathscr{V}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{V}_{n}]
by
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
. The differential is similar to (
2.5
), except we use the weight
n_{w_{i}}(\phi)
for the variable
\mathscr{U}_{i}
, and the weight of
n_{z_{i}}(\phi)
for the variable
\mathscr{V}_{i}
. In general,
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}_{\full}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
is a
curved chain complex
, i.e.
\partial^{2}=\omega_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\id
, for some
\omega_{\mathbb{L}}\in\mathbb{F}[\mathscr{U}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{U}_{n},\mathscr{V}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{V}_{n}]
; see
[
52
, Lemma 2.1]
.

2.3.
Homological actions

Ozsváth and Szabó describe an action of
\Lambda^{*}(H_{1}(Y)/\mathrm{Tors})
on the homology group
\HF^{-}(Y,w,\mathfrak{s})
; see
[
39
, Section 4.2.5]
. For a multi-pointed 3-manifold
(Y,\mathbf{w})
, there is an analogous action of the relative homology group
H_{1}(Y,\bm{\mathbf{w}})
on
\CF^{-}(Y,\mathbf{w},\mathfrak{s})

[
51
]
. See
[
34
]
for a similar description on
\widehat{\HF}(Y,\bm{\mathbf{w}})
. In this section, we recall the construction and describe some analogs on link Floer homology.

If
(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}})
is a multi-pointed Heegaard diagram, and
\lambda
is a path which connects two distinct basepoints
w_{1},w_{2}\in\bm{\mathbf{w}}
, then there is a
relative homology action

A_{\lambda}
, which is an endomorphism of
\CF^{-}(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\mathfrak{s})
and satisfies

(2.6)

A_{\lambda}\partial+\partial A_{\lambda}=U_{1}+U_{2}.

See
[
51
, Lemma 5.1]
.

The map
A_{\lambda}
is defined via the formula

(2.7)

A_{\lambda}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{\bm{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\bm{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}a(\lambda,\phi)\#(\mathcal{M}(\phi)/\mathbb{R})U_{1}^{n_{w_{1}}(\phi)}\cdots U_{n}^{n_{w_{n}}(\phi)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{y}}.

Here
a(\lambda,\phi)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
is a quantity determined as follows. Homotope the path
\lambda
so that it is an immersed curve in
\Sigma
, transverse to the
\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{\beta}}
curves. Then
a(\lambda,\phi)
is obtained by summing the total change of the class
\phi
across the
\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}
curves, as one travels along
\lambda
; compare
[
51
, Section 5.1]
.

If
(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
is a multi-pointed Heegaard link diagram, and
\lambda
connects two basepoints
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, there is an analogous map
A_{\lambda}
on the link Floer homology. In contrast to (
2.6
), we have

(2.8)

A_{\lambda}\partial+\partial A_{\lambda}=\mathscr{U}_{1}\mathscr{V}_{1}+\mathscr{U}_{2}\mathscr{V}_{2},

where
\mathscr{V}_{1}
denotes the variable for the basepoint
z_{1}
which immediately follows
w_{1}
with respect to the ordering of basepoints on the link, and similarly
\mathscr{V}_{2}
is the variable for the basepoint
z_{2}
which immediately follows
w_{2}
. The proof follows from the same strategy as
[
51
, Lemma 5.1]
: one counts the ends of index 2 families of holomorphic disks. There are two types of ends, pairs of index 1 holomorphic disks as well as index 2 boundary degenerations. Pairs of index 1 holomorphic disks contribute the left-hand side of (
2.8
), while the count of boundary degenerations, weighted by
a(\lambda,\phi)
, contributes the right-hand side.

If
z_{i}\in\bm{\mathbf{z}}
, then there is an endomorphism of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}_{\full}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
defined by the formula

\Psi_{z_{i}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\mathscr{V}_{i}^{-1}\sum_{\bm{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\bm{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}n_{z_{i}}(\phi)\#(\mathcal{M}(\phi)/\mathbb{R})\mathscr{U}_{1}^{n_{w_{1}}(\phi)}\cdots\mathscr{U}_{n}^{n_{w_{n}}(\phi)}\mathscr{V}_{1}^{n_{z_{1}}(\phi)}\cdots\mathscr{V}_{n}^{n_{z_{n}}(\phi)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{y}}.

We call
\Psi_{z_{i}}
the
basepoint action
of
z_{i}
. Given
w_{i}\in\bm{\mathbf{w}}
, there is an analogous endomorphism
\Phi_{w_{i}}
. The map
\Psi_{z_{i}}
satisfies

\Psi_{z_{i}}\partial+\partial\Psi_{z_{i}}=\mathscr{U}_{j}+\mathscr{U}_{j+1},

where
w_{j}
and
w_{j+1}
are the
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
basepoints adjacent to
z_{i}
on the link. In particular, if we identify all of the
\mathscr{U}_{i}
variables to a single
\mathscr{U}
, then
\Psi_{z_{i}}
is a chain map. See
[
48
, Lemma 4.1]
or
[
52
, Lemma 3.1]
. Similarly, if
z_{i}
is on a link component which has only one other basepoint, then
\Psi_{z_{i}}
is also a chain map.

2.4.
Heegaard Floer homology of a knotification

Definition 2.9

(Knotification)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=L_{1}\cup\dots\cup L_{n}
be a null-homologous link in a 3-manifold
Y
.

(1)

A
partial knotification
of
\mathcal{L}
with respect to components
L_{i},L_{j}

is a
(n-1)
-component null-homologous link
\mathcal{L}_{ij}
in
Y\#S^{2}\times S^{1}
obtained by connecting
L_{i}
and
L_{j}
with an oriented band going across the
S^{2}\times S^{1}
summand.

(2)

A
knotification
of
\mathcal{L}
is a knot
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
in
Y\#^{n-1}S^{2}\times S^{1}
obtained by consecutive partial knotifications.

The isotopy type
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
does not depend on the choices made; see
[
38
, Propostion 2.1]
.

Suppose
\mathbb{L}=(\mathcal{L},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
is an
n
-component link in
\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1}
, equipped with
2n
basepoints, and
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
is a multi-pointed link in
\#^{m+1}S^{2}\times S^{1}
, obtained by knotifying the components
L_{n-1}
and
L_{n}
of
\mathcal{L}
. Furthermore, we assume that the basepoints on the link components
L_{1},\dots,L_{n-2}
are unchanged in
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
, and on
L_{n-1}^{\prime}
we have only the two basepoints
w_{n}
and
z_{n-1}
. There are two natural maps

\begin{split}F&\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1},\mathbb{L})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\#^{m+1}S^{2}\times S^{1},\mathbb{L}^{\prime})\\
G&\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\#^{m+1}S^{2}\times S^{1},\mathbb{L}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1},\mathbb{L}).\end{split}

The map
F
is the link cobordism map for a 4-dimensional 1-handle, followed by a saddle which crosses over the 1-handle. The decoration on the saddle consists of an arc, which connects the two link components of
\mathbb{L}
. Outside of the saddle region, the decoration consists of “vertical” arcs which connect
\mathbb{L}
to
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
. See the left-hand side of
[
53
, Figure 5.1]
. The map
G
is the map for the link cobordism obtained by reversing the orientation and turning around the above cobordism for
F
.

The following is a key lemma which we use to compute the
H_{1}
action for knotified knots:

Proposition 2.10
.

Suppose
\mathbb{L}
,
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
,
F
and
G
are as above. Let
\lambda
be an arc in
\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1}
which connects the
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
basepoints of
L_{n-1}
and
L_{n}
. Let
\gamma
be the unique element of
H_{1}(\#^{m+1}S^{2}\times S^{1})
obtained by joining the ends of
\lambda
across the 1-handle used in knotification. We have the following:

(a)

F
and
G
are homogeneously graded chain homotopy inverses.

(b)

The map
F
satisfies

F(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{n}})\simeq F(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{n}})\simeq A_{\gamma}F.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, we will describe the case when
\mathcal{L}
is a link in
S^{3}
with two components
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. We begin with claim (a). The proof is formally identical to the proof of
[
53
, Proposition 5.1]
and follows from two 4-dimensional surgery relations
[
53
, Propositions 5.2 and 5.4]
.

We now move onto claim (b). We first show that

(2.11)

F(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}})\simeq A_{\gamma}F.

By definition, we may take

(2.12)

F=S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}F_{1},

where
F_{1}
is the 1-handle map,
S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}
is a quasi-destabilization map, and
F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
is a type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
saddle map;
see
[
55
]
for precise definitions of the relevant maps.

We have now

(2.13)

F_{1}(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}})=(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}})F_{1}

by the same argument that the 1-handle is a chain map
[
41
, Section 4.3]
(see also
[
51
, Lemma 8.11]
). Analogously, the computation of the quasi-stabilized differential in
[
52
, Proposition 5.3]
implies that

A_{\gamma}S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}=S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}A_{\gamma}.

Hence, it is sufficient to show that

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Phi_{z_{1}})=A_{\gamma}F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}.

We recall the definition of the map
F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
. We pick a Heegaard triple
(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
subordinate to the band
[
55
, Defintion 6.2]
. The diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
contains two canonical intersection points,
\Theta^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}
and
\Theta^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}
, where
\Theta^{\bm{\mathbf{o}}}_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}
is the top degree generator with respect to the
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{o}}}
-grading, where
\bm{\mathbf{o}}\in\{\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}}\}
. By definition

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=F_{\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}).

Counting the ends of Maslov index 1 families of holomorphic triangles, weighted by
a(\lambda,\psi)
, we obtain the relation

(2.14)

\begin{split}&F_{\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(A_{\lambda}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}),\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+A_{\lambda}(F_{\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})\\
=&F_{\lambda}^{A}(\partial\bm{\mathbf{x}},\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+F_{\lambda}^{A}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\partial\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+\partial F_{\lambda}^{A}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}});\end{split}

see
[
51
, Lemma 5.2]
. Here
F_{\lambda}^{A}
counts index 0 holomorphic triangles with an extra factor of
a(\lambda,\psi)
. Note that one might expect an extra term involving
F_{\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},A_{\lambda}\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
, however this term vanishes, since
A_{\lambda}
weights disks based on their changes across the
\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}
curves, and
\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta^{\prime}}
.

Similarly, counting the ends of index 1 families of holomorphic triangles, weighted by
n_{z_{2}}(\psi)
, we obtain

(2.15)

\begin{split}&F_{\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}),\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+F_{\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}}(\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}))+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}}(F_{\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}))\\
=&F^{\prime}(\partial\bm{\mathbf{x}},\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+F^{\prime}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\partial\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+\partial F^{\prime}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}),\end{split}

where
F^{\prime}
counts index 0 triangles weighted by a factor of
n_{z_{1}}(\psi)
.

We now claim that

(2.16)

[\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}}(\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})]=0,

where the brackets denote the induced element of homology. Since the map
\Psi_{z_{2}}
and the element
\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
are diagram independent, we may check the relation in (
2.16
) on a genus 0 diagram for an unknot with 4-basepoints; see Figure
1
. On this diagram,
\Psi_{z_{2}}(\Theta_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=0
.

Figure 1.
An unkot with 4 basepoints. The dashed arc is
\lambda
.

Combining (
2.14
), (
2.15
) with (
2.16
), we obtain

(2.17)

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}})\simeq(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}})F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}.

Next, consider a path
\lambda^{\prime}
from
w_{1}
to
w_{2}
, which is a subarc of
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
. We choose
\lambda^{\prime}
so that it is oriented from
w_{1}
to
w_{2}
. There are two such subarcs of
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
, and we pick the one so that the portion of
\lambda^{\prime}
nearest to
w_{1}
is in the beta-handlebody (equivalently, we pick the one which goes over
B
before arriving at a
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
basepoint). Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\lambda^{\prime}
crosses over
z_{2}
. See Figure
2
. We define

\gamma:=\lambda*\lambda^{\prime},

where
*
denotes concatenation.

Figure 2.
The configuration of the band
B
, the basepoints, and the arc
\lambda^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
.

On the Heegaard diagram, we may choose
\lambda^{\prime}
to cross only the alpha curves between
w_{1}
and
z_{2}
, and only the beta curves between
z_{2}
and
w_{2}
. Clearly,

a(\lambda^{\prime},\phi)=n_{w_{2}}(\phi)-n_{z_{2}}(\phi).

Hence,
A_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}}
, or equivalently

(2.18)

\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}}=A_{\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}}.

Combining (
2.17
) and (
2.18
),
we obtain

(2.19)

\begin{split}F(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}})&\simeq S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}(A_{\lambda}+A_{\lambda^{\prime}}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}})F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}F_{1}\\
&\simeq S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}(A_{\gamma}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}})F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}F_{1}\\
&\simeq A_{\gamma}S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}F_{1}.\end{split}

The second line of (
2.19
) follows from the relation
A_{\gamma}\simeq A_{\lambda}+A_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. The final line follows from (
2.13
), as well as the relation that
S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}\Phi_{w_{2}}\simeq S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{+}S_{w_{2},z_{1}}^{-}\simeq 0
by
[
55
, Lemmas 4.11 and 4.13]
, completing the proof of (
2.11
).

Finally, to see that

F(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}})\simeq A_{\gamma}F,

it is sufficient to show that
\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}}\simeq\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}}
on
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{L})
. To see this, we note that on a diagram for
\mathbb{L}
, we can consider a shadow of the link component
L_{2}
. The arc
L_{2}\setminus\{w_{2},z_{2}\}
contains two subarcs, one of which intersects only the alpha curves, and one of which intersects only the beta curves. Hence
a(L_{2},\phi)=n_{w_{2}}(\phi)-n_{z_{2}}(\phi)
for any class of disks
\phi
. On the other hand, this implies that the homology action associated to
0=[L_{2}]\in H_{1}(S^{3})
satisfies

0\simeq A_{L_{2}}=\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}}+\mathscr{V}\Psi_{z_{2}},

completing the proof.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

The homology action on full knotifications may be computed by iterating the above result, via the following lemma:

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
\mathbb{L}
,
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
,
F
and
G
be as in Proposition
2.10
.

(1)

Suppose that
\gamma\in H_{1}(\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1})
. Write
\gamma
also for the induced element of
H_{1}(\#^{m+1}S^{2}\times S^{1})
. Then
A_{\gamma}
commutes with
F
and
G
up to chain homotopy.

(2)

If
\lambda
is an arc in
\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1}
which connects two components of
L_{1},\dots,L_{n-2}
, then the relative homology map
A_{\lambda}
commutes with
F
and
G
up to chain homotopy.

(3)

If
w
and
z
are basepoints on one of the link components
L_{1},\dots,L_{n-2}
, then
\Phi_{w}
and
\Psi_{z}
commute with
F
and
G
up to chain homotopy.

The proof of Lemma
2.20
is similar to the proof of Proposition
2.10
(though strictly easier), and hence we omit it. We refer the reader to
[
51
, Section 5]
and
[
55
, Section 4]
for related results.

2.5.
Generalized correction terms of Levine and Ruberman

Suppose
Y
is an oriented closed three-dimensional manifold. The module
\HF^{\infty}(Y)
is
standard
if for each torsion
\Spin^{c}
structure
{{\mathfrak{s}}}
,

\HF^{\infty}(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}})\cong\Lambda^{\ast}H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}[U,U^{-1}]

as
\Lambda^{\ast}(H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})/\textup{Tors})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}[U]
-modules.
Any manifold
Y
for which the triple cup product vanishes is standard, see
[
31
]
(and also
[
29
, Theorem 3.2]
).
In particular, a connected sum of finitely many copies of
S^{1}\times S^{2}
has standard
\hf^{\infty}
. Hence, a large surgery on a null-homologous
knot in
\#S^{1}\times S^{2}
has standard
\hf^{\infty}
; see
[
43
]
. This means that essentially all 3-manifolds we are going to consider have
standard
\hf^{\infty}
.

There is an action (up to homotopy) of
\Lambda^{*}(H_{1}(Y)/\Tors)
on
\CF^{-}(Y,\mathfrak{s})
. Expanding on work of Ozsváth and Szabo
[
43
]
, Levine and Ruberman
[
29
]
associate a
d
-invariant to any primitive subspace
V
of
H_{1}(Y)/\Tors
(recall that a
primitive subspace
is a free submodule whose quotient is free)
and any
\Spin^{c}
structure
{{\mathfrak{s}}}
on
Y
whose first Chern class is torsion as long as
\HF^{\infty}(Y)
is standard.We denote this invariant by
d(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}},V)
.
For our purposes, the two most important instances are the invariants

d_{\mathrm{bot}}(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}}):=d(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}},H_{1}(Y)/\Tors),\ \ d_{\mathrm{top}}(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}}):=d(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}},\{0\}),

which correspond approximately to the kernel and cokernel, respectively of the
H_{1}(Y)/\Tors
action.

The key property of these invariants is the following inequality, generalizing the Ozsváth–Szabo inequality.

Theorem 2.21

(see
[
29
, Theorem 4.7]
)
.

Suppose
X
is a connected four-manifold such that
b_{2}^{+}(X)=0
and
\partial X=Y
. Suppose
{{\mathfrak{s}}}
is a Spin
c
structure on
Y
that extends to a Spin
c
structure
{{\mathfrak{t}}}

on
X
. Then

d(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}},V)\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(c_{1}^{2}({{\mathfrak{t}}})+b_{2}^{-}(X)\right)+\frac{1}{2}b_{1}(Y)-\rk V,

if
V
contains
\ker\left(H_{1}(Y)/\textup{Tors}\to H_{1}(X)/\textup{Tors}\right)
.

2.6.
V
-invariants

The aim of this section is to gather several definitions of
V_{s}
-invariants. In the context of Heegaard Floer theory, all these definitions lead to the same invariants.

The first definition recalls the classical
V_{s}
-invariant if
C_{*}
is the knot Floer complex
\cfk^{-}
.

Definition 2.22

(
V_{s}
-invariants for complexes over
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,U^{-1}]
)
.

Suppose
C_{*}
is a filtered chain complex of free
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U]
modules (with multiplication by
U
decreasing the filtration level by
1

and the grading by
2
) such that the homology of the localized complex

U^{-1}C_{*}
is equal to
F_{2}[U,U^{-1}]
. For
s\in\mathbb{Z}
the invariant
V_{s}(C_{*})
is such that
-2V_{s}(C_{*})
is the maximal grading
of an element
x\in C_{*}
at filtration level
\leq s
, and the class of
U^{k}x
is non-zero in
H_{*}(C_{*})
for all
k\geq 0
.

Next, we define the
V_{s}
-invariants of a bigraded
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-module where
\mathscr{R}^{-}=\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V}]
. The definition is essentially taken from
[
54
, Equation (10.3)]
. Suppose
C_{*}
is a bigraded chain complex over
\mathscr{R}^{-}

such that multiplication by
\mathscr{U}
changes the grading by
(-2,0)
and multiplication
by
\mathscr{V}
changes the grading by
(0,-2)
.
Let
(\gr_{w},\gr_{z})
denoting the bigrading.

Definition 2.23

(
V_{s}
-invariants over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
)
.

Suppose
C_{*}
is a chain complex over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
such that

(2.24)

(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V})^{-1}\cdot H_{*}(C_{*})\cong\mathscr{R}^{\infty}=\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V},\mathscr{U}^{-1},\mathscr{V}^{-1}],

as bigraded groups. (Here
(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V})^{-1}\cdot
denotes localization at the non-zero monomials of
\mathscr{R}^{-}
). We write
\mathscr{A}_{s}(C_{\ast})
for the subcomplex of
C_{*}
which has
\gr_{w}-\gr_{z}=2s
. We can view
\mathscr{A}_{s}(C_{\ast})
as a complex over
\mathbb{F}[U]
, where
U
acts by
\mathscr{U}\mathscr{V}
. We define
d(\mathscr{A}_{s}(C_{\ast}))
for the maximal
\gr_{w}
-grading of a homogeneously graded,
\mathbb{F}[U]
-non-torsion element of
H_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(C_{\ast}))
. We define

V_{s}(C_{\ast})=-\frac{1}{2}d(\mathscr{A}_{s}(C_{\ast})).

Remark 2.25
.

Suppose
M
is a graded module over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
such that
(\mathscr{U}^{-1},\mathscr{V}^{-1})\cdot M\cong\mathscr{R}^{\infty}
as bigraded groups. We define

V_{s}(M)
to be the
V_{s}(C_{*})
with
C_{*}
being the chain complex with the same underlying module structure as
M
but trivial differential.

Remark 2.26
.

If
C_{*}
is the chain complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(S^{3},K)
for a knot
K\subset S^{3}
,
V_{s}(C_{*})
is the classical
V
-function of the knot
K
. In this case, we also denote it by
V_{s}(K)
if the context is clear. We refer the readers to
[
54
, Section 1.5]
for translating between the chain complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(S^{3},K)
and
\CFK^{-}(S^{3},K)
.

Suppose
C_{*}
is as in Definition
2.23
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
. The chain complex
C_{*}\{a,b\}
is defined as the chain complex
equal to
C_{*}
, but with grading
shifted by
(a,b)
. That is, if
x\in C_{*}
has bigrading
(c,d)
, then
x\in C_{*}\{a,b\}
has bigrading
(a+c,b+d)
.

Lemma 2.27
.

Suppose
C_{*}
is a bigraded chain complex over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
and let
D_{*}=C_{*}\{a,b\}
be the chain complex with shifted grading.
Then
V_{s+(a-b)/2}(D_{*})=V_{s}(C_{*})-a/2
.

Proof.

We use the fact that
\mathscr{A}_{s}(C_{*})=\mathscr{A}_{s+(a-b)/2}(D_{*})
.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

In our computations, we will need to show that
V_{s}
-invariants of locally equivalent complexes are the same. We recall the relevant definition.

Definition 2.28
.

Two chain complexes
C_{*}
and
D_{*}
are
locally equivalent
, if there exist grading preserving,
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-equivariant chain maps
f\colon C_{*}\to D_{*}
,
g\colon D_{*}\to C_{*}

such that both
f
and
g
induce the identity map on

(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V})^{-1}\cdot C_{*}\cong(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V})^{-1}\cdot D_{*}
.

We have the following result (see
[
53
, Section 2]
,
[
21
]
or
[
24
, Section 3]
):

Proposition 2.29
.

(a)

If
C_{*}
is locally equivalent to
D_{*}
, then
V_{s}(C_{*})=V_{s}(D_{*})
for all
s
.

(b)

If
C_{*}
is locally equivalent to
D_{*}
and
E_{*}
is locally equivalent to
F_{*}
, then
C_{*}\otimes E_{*}
is locally equivalent
to
D_{*}\otimes F_{*}
.

We now extend Definition
2.23
to the case of chain complexes with a group action.
Suppose
C_{*}
is a bigraded chain complex over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
and
H
is a free abelian group such that the ring
\Lambda^{*}H
acts on
H_{*}(C_{*})
, and the action of
H
has degree
(-1,-1)
. Let
\Tors\subset H_{*}(C_{*})
denote the
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-torsion submodule. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{top}}

\displaystyle=\coker\left(H\otimes(H_{*}(C_{*})/\Tors)\to(H_{*}(C_{*})/\Tors)\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{bot}}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\gamma\in H}\ker(\gamma\colon(H_{*}(C_{*})/\Tors)\to(H_{*}(C_{*})/\Tors)).

By analogy of (
2.24
) we require that

(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V})^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{top}}\cong\mathscr{R}^{\infty}\cong(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V})^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{bot}}

as relatively bigraded
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-modules. Let
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\mathrm{bot}}
) denote the
\mathbb{F}[U]
-submodule generated by homogeneously graded elements
x\in\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{top}}
(resp.
x\in\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{bot}})
such that
\gr_{w}(x)-\gr_{z}(x)=2s
(recall
U
acts by
\mathscr{U}\mathscr{V}
). We define
d^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}(C_{*})
to be the maximal
\gr_{w}
-grading of a homogeneously graded,
\mathbb{F}[U]
non-torsion element of
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}
, and we define
d^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}(C_{*})
analogously.

Definition 2.30
.

We set

V_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}(C_{*}):=-\frac{1}{2}d^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}(C_{*})\qquad\text{and}\qquad V_{s}^{\mathrm{bot}}(C_{*})=-\frac{1}{2}d^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}(C_{*}).

Remark 2.31
.

If
K
is a null-homologous knot in a closed, oriented connected
3
-manifold
Y
with standard
HF^{\infty}(Y)
, for simplicity, we use
\mathscr{A}_{s}(K)
to denote
\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,K))
, and use
V_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}(K),V_{s}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K)
to denote
V_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,K))
and
V_{s}^{\mathrm{bot}}(\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,K))
, repsectively.

2.7.
Large surgery formula

To set up the notation, we recall the large surgery formula
[
39
, Section 4]
and relate the
d
-invariants of the surgery on a knot to its
V_{s}
-invariants. We first recall the description of Spin
c
structures on a surgery.

Definition 2.32
.

Suppose
Y
is a closed 3-manifold and
K\subset Y
is a null-homologous knot. Let
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(Y)
, and
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. For any
m\in[-q/2,q/2)\cap\mathbb{Z}

we denote by
{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m}
the unique Spin
c
structure on
Y_{q}(K)
such that
{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m}
extends to a Spin
c
structure
{{\mathfrak{t}}}_{m}
on
W
uniquely
characterized by the properties that
\mathfrak{t}_{m}|_{Y}=\mathfrak{s}
and
\langle c_{1}({{\mathfrak{t}}}_{m}),F\rangle+q=2m
, where
W
is the trace of the surgery on
K
and
F
is the generator of
H_{2}(W)

obtained by gluing a Seifert surface for
K
with the core of the two-handle.

With this notation, we state Ozsváth and Szabó’s large surgery theorem
[
39
, Theorem 4.1]
:

Theorem 2.33
.

Suppose
K\subset Y
is a null-homologous knot in a closed 3-manifold. Suppose
q>2g_{3}(K)
is an integer. For a Spin
c
structure

{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m}
on
Y
as in Definition
2.32
, there exists a quasi-isomorphism between
\CF^{-}(Y_{q}(K),{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m})

and
\mathscr{A}_{m}
, where
\mathscr{A}_{m}
is a
\mathbb{F}[U]
subcomplex of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s})
of elements
x
with grading
\gr_{w}(x)-\gr_{z}(x)=2m
. If
\mathfrak{s}
is torsion, then the quasi-isomorphism shifts the grading
(
Maslov grading on
\CF^{-}(Y_{q}(K),{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m})
and
\gr_{w}
-grading on
\mathscr{A}_{m}
)
by
\tfrac{(q-2m)^{2}-q}{4q}
.

From this theorem we obtain the following well-known equalities.

Theorem 2.34
.

Suppose
K\subset Y
is as in Theorem
2.33
and
q>2g_{3}(K)
.

(a)

If
Y
is a rational homology sphere, then
d(Y_{q}(K),{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m})=\tfrac{(q-2m)^{2}-q}{4q}-2V_{m}(K)
;

(b)

If
b_{1}(Y)>0
and
\HF^{\infty}(Y)
is standard, then
d^{\mathrm{top}}(Y_{q}(K),{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m})=\tfrac{(q-2m)^{2}-q}{4q}-2V^{\mathrm{top}}_{m}(K)
and
d^{\mathrm{bot}}(Y_{q}(K),{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{m})=\tfrac{(q-2m)^{2}-q}{4q}-2V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{m}(K)
.

3.
Topology of complex curves and their neighborhoods

In this section we give a precise definition of the notion of a tubular neighborhood of a possibly singular curve in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
. We describe the boundary of this neighborhood
in terms of the surgery on a link. We perform several helpful algebro-topological computations.

3.1.
‘Tubular’ neighborhood of a complex curve

Let
C\subset\mathbb{C}P^{2}
be a reduced complex curve of degree
d
. We do not insist that
C
is irreducible. We write
C_{1},\dots,C_{e}
for the irreducible components of
C
and let
d_{1},\dots,d_{e}
(resp.
g_{1},\dots,g_{e}
)
denote their degrees (resp. genera). Hereafter by the
genus

g(C)
of a complex curve we understand the genus of its normalization, that is, the geometric genus. From the topological perspective, the geometric genus of a singular curve is the sum of genera of connected components of the smooth locus of the curve, regarded as an open surface.

We denote by
z_{1},\dots,z_{u}
the singular points of
C
. For each such singular point
z_{i}
we denote by
r_{i}
the number of branches. We write
\mathcal{L}_{i}
for the link of singularity, whose components are
L_{i1},\dots,L_{ir_{i}}
. We choose once
and for all pairwise disjoint closed balls
B_{1},\dots,B_{u}
with centers respectively
z_{1},\dots,z_{u}
and such that
C\cap\partial B_{i}
is the link
\mathcal{L}_{i}
and
C\cap B_{i}
is homeomorphic to a cone over
\mathcal{L}_{i}
.

As the curve
C
is not smoothly embedded at its singular points, the notion of a tubular neighborhood of
C
requires some clarification. The following is
an extension of the construction of
[
5
]
.

Take a tubular neighborhood
N_{0}
in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}\setminus(B_{1}\cup\dots\cup B_{u})
of the smooth part

C_{0}:=C\setminus(B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{u})
. We define
N
to be the union of
N_{0}
and
B_{1},\dots,B_{u}
.
Define

(3.1)

\rho=2g-e+1+\sum_{i=1}^{u}(r_{i}-1).

We now provide a surgery theoretical description of
N
and its boundary
Y
. We define a 3-manifold
Z
containing a link
\mathcal{L}
, as follows. We begin with the disjoint union
\mathcal{L}_{0}:=\mathcal{L}_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup\mathcal{L}_{u}
in
Z_{0}:=S^{3}\sqcup\dots\sqcup S^{3}
. Next, we pick a collection of pairwise disjoint and properly embedded arcs
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\rho+u-1}
on
C_{0}
which form a basis of
H_{1}(C_{0},\partial C_{0})
. Such a basis cuts
C_{0}
into a union of disks. We let
Z=\#^{\rho}S^{2}\times S^{1}
be the boundary of the 4-manifold
H
obtained by attaching
\rho+u-1
4-dimensional 1-handles to
\partial(B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{u})=Z_{0}
, each containing a 2-dimensional band (corresponding to a
\lambda_{i}
), which we attach to
\mathcal{L}_{0}
. We let
\mathcal{L}=L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{e}\subset Z
be the resulting link. We think of this operation as a generalization of the knotification and connected sum operations of knots. By construction,
\mathcal{L}
is an
e
-component link inside of the connected sum of
\rho
copies of
S^{1}\times S^{2}
. Furthermore, each component of
\mathcal{L}
is null-homologous.

We have the following (compare
[
4
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
1
, Lemma 3.1]
):

Proposition 3.2
.

The 3-manifold
Y=\partial N
is the surgery on
\mathcal{L}\subset Z
with surgery coefficients
(d_{1}^{2},\dots,d_{e}^{2})
. The 4-manifold
N

is obtained by attaching
e
2-handles to the boundary connected sum of
\rho
copies of
D^{3}\times S^{1}
.

Proof.

The fact that
N
is obtained by attaching
e
2-handles to
Z
along
\mathcal{L}
follows from the fact that the arcs
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\rho+u-1}
cut
C_{0}
into a collection of disks. The claim about the framings follows immediately from the fact that
C_{i}
has self-intersection
d_{i}^{2}
.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Remark 3.3
.

If
e=1
,
\mathcal{L}
is a knot. This knot can be obtained as a connected sum of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\dots,\widehat{\mathcal{L}_{u}}

and
g
copies of the Borromean knot. Here
the hat denotes knotification.

3.2.
Algebraic topology

In this section, we describe some basic algebro-topological facts about the tubular neighborhood
N
, and its boundary
Y
. Our description of
\Spin^{c}
structures is similar to the one described in
[
32
, Section 11.1]
.

Let
\Lambda=\{d_{i}^{2}\}_{i=1}^{e}
denote the integral framing on
\mathcal{L}\subset Z
, described in the last section, and let
\Xi=\{d_{i}d_{j}\}_{i,j=1}^{e}
denote the framing matrix. We let
W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L})
denote the 2-handle cobordism from
Z
to
Y
. Recall that
N
is the union of a 1-handlebody,
H
, and
W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L})
.

There is a map

(3.4)

\mathcal{F}\colon H^{2}(W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L}))\to\mathbb{Z}^{e}\oplus H^{2}(Z),

given by

\mathcal{F}(c)=(\langle c,[\hat{F}_{1}]\rangle,\dots,\langle c,[\hat{F}_{e}]\rangle,c|_{Z}).

Here
\hat{F}_{i}
is the surface obtained by capping a Seifert surface for
L_{i}
in
Z
with the core of the 2-handle. An easy argument
involving Mayer-Vietoris
sequence on the handle attachement regions in
Z
shows that
\mathcal{F}
is an isomorphism.

Dually, we may view
W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L})
as being obtained by attaching 2-handles to a link
\mathcal{L}^{*}
in
Y
. We consider the Mayer-Vietoris sequence obtained by viewing
W_{\Lambda}
as the union of
[0,1]\times Y
and
e
2-handles.
A portion of this exact sequence reads

H^{1}(\mathcal{L}^{*})\to H^{2}(W_{\Lambda}(Y))\to H^{2}(Y)\to 0.

In particular,
H^{2}(Y)
is the quotient of
H^{2}(W_{\Lambda}(Y))
by the image of
H^{1}(\mathcal{L}^{*})
. Furthermore, from the definition of the coboundary map in the Mayer-Vietoris exact sequence, an element of
H^{1}(\mathcal{L}^{*})
acts by the Poincaré duals of the cores of the 2-handles attached along
\mathcal{L}
. Using the isomorphism
\mathcal{F}
from (
3.4
), we thus obtain

(3.5)

H^{2}(Y)\cong(\mathbb{Z}^{e}/\im(\Xi))\oplus H^{2}(Z).

There are analogous descriptions for
\Spin^{c}
structures on
Y
and
W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L})
, as follows. Consider the map

(3.6)

\mathcal{T}_{W}\colon\Spin^{c}(W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L}))\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}^{e}\times\Spin^{c}(Z),

given by

\mathcal{T}_{W}(\mathfrak{s})=\left(\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}),[\hat{F}_{1}]\rangle-[\hat{F}]\cdot[\hat{F}_{1}]}{2},\dots,\frac{\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}),[\hat{F}_{e}]\rangle-[\hat{F}]\cdot[\hat{F}_{e}]}{2},\mathfrak{s}|_{Z}\right),

where
[\hat{F}]
is the sum of the
[\hat{F}_{i}]
. Similar to the argument for cohomology, an easy application of Mayer-Vietoris shows that
\mathcal{T}_{W}
is an isomorphism onto its image.
Since
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
is a characteristic vector,

\langle c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}),[\hat{F}_{i}]\rangle-[\hat{F}_{i}]^{2}
is even as well.
Using this, it is not hard to identify the image of
\mathcal{T}_{W}
as
\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{L})\times\Spin^{c}(Z)
, where
\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{L})
is affine lattice in
\mathbb{Q}^{e}
generated by tuples
(a_{1},\dots,a_{e})
where

a_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\lk(\mathcal{L}_{i},\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathcal{L}_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}\textrm{ for all $i$}.

A similar argument as for cohomology implies
\Spin^{c}(Y)
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\Spin^{c}(W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L}))
by the action of the Poincaré duals of the cores of the 2-handles attached to
\mathcal{L}
. This translates into the isomorphism

(3.7)

\mathcal{T}_{Y}\colon\Spin^{c}(Y)\cong(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{L})/\im(\Xi))\times\Spin^{c}(Z).

With respect to the isomorphisms
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{T}_{W}
, the Chern class map takes a simple form:

c_{1}(s_{1},\dots,s_{e},\mathfrak{t})=(2s_{1}+[\hat{F}]\cdot[\hat{F}_{1}],\dots,2s_{e}+[\hat{F}]\cdot[\hat{F}_{e}],c_{1}(\mathfrak{t})).

Since
Z=\#^{\rho}S^{2}\times S^{1}
bounds the 1-handlebody
H\subset N
, we know that
\delta(H^{1}(Z))=\{0\}\subset H^{2}(N)
. Hence, a Mayer-Vietoris argument identifies
\Spin^{c}(N)
with the set of
\Spin^{c}
structures on
W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L})
which extend over
H
, or equivalently the ones which have torsion restriction to
Z
. Hence,

\Spin^{c}(N)\cong\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{L}).

The following is helpful for understanding
H^{2}(Y)
:

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose
\Xi=\{a_{ij}\}_{i,j=1}^{e}
is a matrix such that
a_{ij}=d_{i}d_{j}
, for some non-zero integers
d_{i}
. Then
\mathbb{Z}^{e}/\im(\Xi)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{e-1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/\theta^{2}
, where
\theta=\gcd(d_{1},\dots,d_{e})
. .

Proof.

Note that
\im(\Xi)
is the span of
\theta(d_{1},\dots,d_{e})^{T}
. By module theory over a principal ideal domain, we have
\mathbb{Z}^{e}/\im(\Xi)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{e-1}\oplus\Tors(\mathbb{Z}^{e}/\im(\Xi))
. By definition,
\Tors(\mathbb{Z}^{e}/\im(\Xi))
is generated by the set of vectors
v
in
\mathbb{Z}^{e}
such that
n[v]=m[\theta(d_{1},\dots,d_{e})^{T}]
for some integers
n
and
m
. Clearly,
\Tors(\mathbb{Z}^{e}/\im(\Xi))
is generated by the vector
(d_{1}/\theta,\dots,d_{e}/\theta)^{T}
, which has order
\theta^{2}
. The proof is complete.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Combining Lemma
3.8
with equation (
3.5
), we conclude that

(3.9)

b_{1}(Y)=e-1+b_{1}(Z)=e-1+\rho.

If
j\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
, let
{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{j}
denote the
\Spin^{c}
structure on
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
which satisfies

(3.10)

\langle c_{1}({{\mathfrak{c}}}_{j}),E\rangle=j,

where
E
is a complex line. In terms of the isomorphism in (
3.7
), we have

(3.11)

\mathcal{T}_{Y}({{\mathfrak{c}}}_{j}|_{Y})=\left(\frac{jd_{1}-d_{1}(d_{1}+\cdots+d_{e})}{2},\dots,\frac{jd_{e}-d_{e}(d_{1}+\cdots+d_{e})}{2},0\right).

We now let
X
denote the complement of the interior of
N
in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

(1)

X
has trivial intersection form.

(2)

Suppose
\mathfrak{s}
is a torsion
\Spin^{c}
structure on
Y
. Then
\mathfrak{s}
extends over
X
if and only if it extends over
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
.

Proof.

The proof follows identical arguments as in
[
4
, Sections 3 and 4]
, therefore we provide only a sketch.
Claim (
1
) follows from the fact that the inclusion map
H_{2}(X)\to H_{2}(\mathbb{C}P^{2})
vanishes, since all elements of
H_{2}(X)
are disjoint from
C
.

Claim (
2
) is proven as follows. A
\Spin^{c}
structure on
Y
always extends over
W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L})
. Furthermore, the isomorphisms in (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) are clearly compatible with the natural restriction maps from
\Spin^{c}(W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L}))
to
\Spin^{c}(Y)
and
\Spin^{c}(Z)
. A
\Spin^{c}
structure on
W_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L})
extends over
N
if and only if it restricts to the torsion
\Spin^{c}
structure on
Z
. Hence, a
\Spin^{c}
structure on
Y
extends over
N
if and only if the
\Spin^{c}
factor on
\Spin^{c}(Z)
in (
3.7
) is torsion. In particular, any torsion
\Spin^{c}
structure on
Y
extends over
N
. Since a
\Spin^{c}
structure on
Y
extends over
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
if and only if it extends over both
X
and
N
, the claim follows.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

3.3.
d
-invariant inequalities for the neigborhood of
C

We are now in position to prove an inequality for the
d
-invariants of boundaries of neighborhoods of complex curves in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
as in Subsection
3.1
.
With the notation from that subsection
we have the following result.

Proposition 3.13
.

For any
\Spin^{c}
structure
{{\mathfrak{s}}}
on
Y
that extends over
X
and whose first Chern class is torsion, we have:

d_{\mathrm{bot}}(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}})\geq-\frac{\rho+e-1}{2},\quad d_{\mathrm{top}}(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}})\leq\frac{\rho+e-1}{2}.

Proof.

By equation (
3.9
), we know that
b_{1}(Y)=\rho+e-1.

The intersection form on
X
is trivial by Lemma
3.12
. From Theorem
2.21
, we obtain

d_{\mathrm{bot}}(Y,\mathfrak{s})=d(Y,\mathfrak{s},H_{1}(Y)/\Tors)\geq-\frac{\rho+e-1}{2},

since the terms involving
c_{1}^{2}
and
b_{2}^{-}(X)
vanish.

Since the intersection form on
X
vanishes, we may reverse the orientation of
X
and
Y
and apply to the same argument to get that

(3.14)

d_{\mathrm{bot}}(-Y,\mathfrak{s})=d(-Y,\mathfrak{s},H_{1}(Y)/\Tors)\geq-\frac{\rho+e-1}{2}.

It follows from
[
29
, Proposition 4.2]
and the fact that
d^{\ast}(Y,\mathfrak{s},H_{1}(Y)/\Tors)=d_{\textrm{top}}(Y,\mathfrak{s})
(see
[
29
, pg. 6]
)

d_{\mathrm{bot}}(-Y,\mathfrak{s})=-d_{\mathrm{top}}(Y,\mathfrak{s}).

Combining this with equation (
3.14
), we conclude that

d_{\mathrm{top}}(Y,\mathfrak{s})\leq\frac{\rho+e-1}{2},

completing the proof.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

4.
Building blocks for computing the
d
-invariants of
Y

In order to effectively use Proposition
3.13
, we need algorithms to compute the
d
-invariants of
Y
.
Our main tool will be the large surgery formula. To use it, we need to understand the Floer chain complexes of various links that are related
to plane curve singularities.

4.1.
The staircase complexes for L-space knots

A knot
K\subset S^{3}
is called an
L-space knot
if there is a positive integer
q
such that
S^{3}_{q}(K)
is an L-space, i.e.
\HF^{-}(S^{3}_{q}(K),\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathbb{F}[U]
for each
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(S^{3}_{q}(K))
.
All algebraic knots are L-space knots; see
[
18
, Theorem 1.10]
.

There is a simple description of Floer chain complexes of L-space knots, due to Ozsváth and Szabó
[
40
, Theorem 1.2]
. (Note that therein, only
\widehat{\HFK}(K)
is described, but the algorithm actually produces a description of
\CFK^{\infty}(K)
.) We describe their algorithm presently. Let
K
be an L-space knot. Ozsváth and Szabó prove that the Alexander polynomial of
K
, which we denote
\Delta_{K}(t)
has the following form:

(4.1)

\Delta_{K}(t)=t^{\alpha_{0}}-t^{\alpha_{1}}+\dots+t^{\alpha_{2r}},

where
0=\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{2r}
. Define the gap function

\beta_{i}:=\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i-1},

for
1\leq i\leq 2r
.

We now describe the complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(K)
over the ring
\mathscr{R}^{-}
. The complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(K)
is freely generated over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
by elements

\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1},\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\cdots,\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2r-1},\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2r}.

The differential takes the following form

(4.2)

\partial(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i})=0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2i+1})=\mathscr{U}^{\beta_{2i+1}}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i}+\mathscr{V}^{\beta_{2i+2}}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i+2}.

The
(\gr_{w},\gr_{z})
-bigradings are determined by the normalization that
\gr_{w}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0})=0
and
\gr_{z}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2r})=0
. Recall that the variable
\mathscr{U}
has bigrading
(-2,0)
, and the variable
\mathscr{V}
has bigrading
(0,-2)
.

The gradings can be expressed in the following way.
Write

\Delta_{K}=1+(t-1)(t^{g_{1}}+\dots+t^{g_{s}})

for some positive integers
g_{1}<\dots<g_{s}
. Define
S_{K}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\{g_{1},\dots,g_{s}\}
, and

(4.3)

R_{K}(k)=\#S_{K}\cap[0,k),\textrm{ if }k\in\mathbb{Z}.

With this notation, the gradings of the
x_{2i}
generator are
\gr_{w}(x_{2i})=-2R(\alpha_{2i})
and
\gr_{z}(x_{2i})=2R(\alpha_{2i})-2g_{3}(K)
; compare

[
5
, Section 4]
.

Remark 4.4
.

If
K
is an algebraic knot, the set
S_{K}
turns out to be a semigroup (note that if
K
is only an L-space knot,
S_{K}
need not be a
semigroup). In fact, this is the semigroup of that singular point.
The function
R_{K}
is the
semigroup counting function
.
Refer to
[
49
, Section 4]
for details on semigroups.

The following corollary is a compilation of
[
5
, Proposition 5.6 and Lemma 6.2]
.

Corollary 4.5
.

The
V_{s}
-invariants of an L–space knot satisfy that
V_{-g+j}(K)=R_{K}(j)-j+g
.

The Künneth formula for the knot Floer chain complex allows us to compute the
V_{j}
-invariants of a connected sum of L-space knots. The
following result is given in
[
5
, Formula (6.3)]
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
K_{1},\dots,K_{n}
be L–space knots. Set
K=K_{1}\#\dots\#K_{n}
and let
g=g_{3}(K)
. Then:

V_{j}(K)+j=R_{K}(g+j),

where
R_{K}=R_{K_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond R_{K_{n}}
is the infimal convolution of
R_{K_{1}},\dots,R_{K_{n}}
.

We recall that if
I,J\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
are two functions bounded from above, their
infimal convolution
is given by
I\diamond J(m)=\min_{i+j=m}I(i)+J(j)
.

A prominent role in the present paper is played by the following complexes.

Definition 4.7
.

Suppose
n\geq 1
. We write
\mathcal{S}^{n}
for the staircase complex built from the Alexander polynomial
\Delta(t)=1-t+\cdots+t^{2n}
. Explicitly, it is generated by elements
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2n-1},\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2n}
with differential
\partial(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i})=0
and

\partial(\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{2i+1})=\mathscr{U}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i}+\mathscr{V}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2i+2}.

The bigradings are given by
(\gr_{w}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{j}),\gr_{z}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{j}))=(-j,j-2n)
, if
j
is even. The same formula holds for
\bm{\mathbf{y}}_{j}
, if
j
is odd.

The complex
\mathcal{S}^{-n}
is defined as
the dual complex to
\mathcal{S}^{n}
. More specifically, it is generated by elements
\underline{\bm{\mathbf{x}}}_{0},\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{1},\dots,\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{2n-1},\underline{\bm{\mathbf{x}}}_{2n}

with differential
\partial(\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{2i+1})=0
,
\partial(\underline{\bm{\mathbf{x}}}_{2i})=\mathscr{V}\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{2i-1}+\mathscr{U}\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{2i+1}
, and the convention that
\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{-1}=\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{2n+1}=0
.
For
j
even, the grading of
\underline{\bm{\mathbf{x}}}_{j}
is
(j,2n-j)
, and an analogous formula holds for the grading of
\underline{\bm{\mathbf{y}}}_{j}
if
j
is odd.

Remark 4.8
.

The complex
\mathcal{S}^{n}
is the
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}
complex of the positive torus knot
T(2,2n+1)
, while
\mathcal{S}^{-n}
is the complex for the negative torus knot
-T(2,2n+1)
.

The following result is proved e.g. in
[
19
, Theorem B.1]
. (Note that
\nu^{+}
-equivalence is equivalent to local equivalence; cf.
[
21
, Proposition 3.11]
.)

Proposition 4.9
.

The tensor product
\mathcal{S}^{k}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{\ell}
is locally equivalent to
\mathcal{S}^{k+\ell}
.

4.2.
The Hopf link

Our next goal is to describe the
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}
complexes for the
T(2,2n)
torus links, their mirrors and their knotifications. As the calculations
are rather involved, we begin with describing the Floer chain complex for the Hopf link
H
, leaving
the general case to Subsection
4.3
. While the complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(H)
is well known (it can be computed explicitly using a very simple diagram),
to the best of our knowledge, the calculation of the action of
H_{1}(S^{2}\times S^{1})

on the knot Floer chain complex of the knotification of
H
is new.

\alpha
\beta
\mathrm{h}_{a}
\mathrm{h}_{b}
\mathrm{h}_{c}
\mathrm{h}_{d}
\lambda
e
w_{1}
z_{2}
z_{1}
w_{2}

Figure 3.
A genus
0
Heegaard diagram for the Hopf link. The thick (red) curve is the
\alpha
-curve, the thin (blue) curve is the
\beta
-curve.
The dotted curve is used to compute the action of
H_{1}(S^{2}\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z})
on the knotification of the Hopf link.

As our main focus will be eventually the knotification of
H
, we restrict our attention to the link Floer
complex over the ring
\mathscr{R}^{-}
, as opposed to the version with a variable for each basepoint.

Consider the diagram for the Hopf link, as in Figure
3
.
The complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(H)
is generated over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
by four elements,
\mathrm{h}_{a}
,
\mathrm{h}_{b}
,
\mathrm{h}_{c}
and
\mathrm{h}_{d}
, which correspond to the intersections of the
\alpha
and
\beta
curves in Figure
3
. The gradings are as follows:

(4.10)

\begin{split}(\gr_{w}(\mathrm{h}_{a}),\gr_{z}(\mathrm{h}_{a}))&=\left(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{3}{2}\right)\\
(\gr_{w}(\mathrm{h}_{c}),\gr_{z}(\mathrm{h}_{c}))&=\left(-\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}(\gr_{w}(\mathrm{h}_{b}),\gr_{z}(\mathrm{h}_{b}))&=\left(-\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)\\
(\gr_{w}(\mathrm{h}_{d}),\gr_{z}(\mathrm{h}_{d}))&=\left(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)\end{split}

The differential in the complex is computed by counting holomorphic disks of Maslov grading zero. Counting bigons shows that

(4.11)

\partial\mathrm{h}_{a}=\partial\mathrm{h}_{c}=0,\ \partial\mathrm{h}_{b}=\partial\mathrm{h}_{d}=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{h}_{a}+\mathscr{V}\mathrm{h}_{c}.

The homology of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(H)
is a direct sum of two copies of
\mathscr{R}^{\infty}
.
One copy is spanned by
[\mathrm{h}_{b}+\mathrm{h}_{d}]
, the other copy is spanned by
[\mathscr{U}\mathrm{h}_{a}]=[\mathscr{V}\mathrm{h}_{c}]
.

We now describe the homology action
A_{\gamma}
on
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(\widehat{H})
, where
\widehat{H}
denotes the knotification of
H
, and
\gamma
is a generator of
H_{1}(S^{2}\times S^{1})
. We will use Proposition
2.10
. The formula therein involves the relative homology action
A_{\lambda}
on
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(H)
, which we compute presently. In our present case, the arc
\lambda
has only one intersection with an alpha curve, which occurs at a point labeled
e
in Figure
3
.
The map
A_{\lambda}
counts holomorphic disks of Maslov index 1, with weights corresponding to changes along the alpha boundary of a disk; see (
2.7
).
Counting bigons with these weights, we obtain:

(4.12)

A_{\lambda}(\mathrm{h}_{a})=\mathscr{V}(\mathrm{h}_{b}+\mathrm{h}_{d}),\ A_{\lambda}(\mathrm{h}_{b})=0,\ A_{\lambda}(\mathrm{h}_{c})=\mathscr{U}(\mathrm{h}_{b}+\mathrm{h}_{d}),\ A_{\lambda}(\mathrm{h}_{d})=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{h}_{a}.

We recall that in Section
2.4
we defined a knotification map

F\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(H)\to\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(\widehat{H}),

which is a homotopy equivalence. In Proposition
2.10
, we showed that

F(A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}})\simeq A_{\gamma}F.

Hence, as a model for the pair
(\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(\widehat{H}),A_{\gamma})
, we may use
(\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(H),A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}})
. Abusing notation slightly, we will write
A_{\gamma}
for the endomorphism of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(H)
given by
A_{\gamma}:=A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}}
.
One easily computes

\Phi_{w_{2}}(\mathrm{h}_{d})=\mathrm{h}_{a},

and
\Phi_{w_{2}}
vanishes on the other generators.
Hence,

(4.13)

A_{\gamma}(\mathrm{h}_{a})=\mathscr{V}(\mathrm{h}_{b}+\mathrm{h}_{d}),\ A_{\gamma}(\mathrm{h}_{b})=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{h}_{a},\ A_{\gamma}(\mathrm{h}_{c})=\mathscr{U}(\mathrm{h}_{b}+\mathrm{h}_{d}),\ A_{\gamma}(\mathrm{h}_{d})=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{h}_{a}.

With a change of basis
\mathrm{h}_{d}^{\prime}=\mathrm{h}_{b}+\mathrm{h}_{d}
, we obtain the following presentation of
(\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(\widehat{H}),A_{\gamma})
:

(4.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{d}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}}

In (
4.14
), the dashed arrows denote differentials, and the solid arrows denote the action of
A_{\gamma}
.

We may obtain a simpler model of the homology action by replacing
A_{\gamma}
with
A_{\gamma}+[\partial,F]
, where
F
is the
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-equivariant map which satisfies

F(\mathrm{h}_{a})=\mathrm{h}_{a},\quad\text{and}\quad F(\mathrm{h}_{b})=F(\mathrm{h}_{c})=F(\mathrm{h}_{d})=0.

The resulting model for
(\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(\widehat{H}),A_{\gamma})
is shown in (
4.15
).

(4.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{d}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}}

4.3.
The torus link
T(2,2n)

Let
L_{n}\subset S^{3}
denote a 2-component link
T(2,2n)
. Note that
L_{1}
is the positive Hopf link. In this subsection, we study the Floer chain complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(L_{n})
as an
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-module. This gives the Floer chain complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(S^{2}\times S^{1},H_{n})
, where
H_{n}
is the knotification of
L_{n}
.

The Heegaard diagram of the link
L_{n}
in
S^{3}
is shown in Figure
4
and Floer chain complex is in Figure
5
. The Heegaard diagram displayed therein is obtained from a doubly pointed open book whose page is a disk, and whose monodromy is
\gamma^{n}
, where
\gamma
denotes a Dehn-twist parallel to the boundary.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Figure 5.
The chain complexes for
T(2,4)
(1st level from top) and
T(2,6)
(2nd level). On the third level is the map
A_{\lambda}
on the complex for
T(2,6)
, and on the bottom is the map
\Phi_{w_{2}}
.

It is easy to see that there are
4n
generators
x_{0},\dots,x_{4n-1}
of the complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(L_{n})
. By counting bigons, one obtains the following formulas for the differential:

(4.16)

\begin{split}\partial x_{i}&=\partial x_{4n-i}=\mathscr{V}(x_{i-1}+x_{4n-i+1})+\mathscr{U}(x_{i+1}+x_{4n-i-1})\quad\textrm{ if $2\leq i\leq 2n-2$}\\
\partial x_{1}&=\partial x_{4n-1}=\mathscr{V}x_{0}+\mathscr{U}(x_{2}+x_{4n-2}),\\
\partial x_{2n-1}&=\partial x_{2n+1}=\mathscr{U}x_{2n}+\mathscr{V}(x_{2n-2}+x_{2n+2}),\\
\partial x_{0}&=\partial x_{2n}=0.\end{split}

It is convenient to do the following bigraded change of basis to the complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(L_{n})
. Namely we consider the basis
x_{1},\dots,x_{2n-1},y_{0},\dots,y_{2n}
, where

(4.17)

\begin{split}y_{i}&=x_{i}+x_{4n-i}\\
y_{0}&=x_{0},\\
y_{2n}&=x_{2n}.\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}&\text{if }\quad 1\leq i\leq 2n-1,\\
&\\
&\,\end{split}

With this change of basis, the differential takes the form

(4.18)

\begin{split}\partial x_{i}&=\mathscr{V}y_{i-1}+\mathscr{U}y_{i+1}\quad\text{if}\quad 1\leq i\leq 2n-1\\
\partial y_{i}&=0.\end{split}

The gradings of the generators in
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(L_{n})
are summarized in the following lemma:

Lemma 4.19
.

If
1\leq i\leq 2n-1
, then

\left(\gr_{w}(x_{i}),\gr_{z}(x_{i})\right)=\left(\gr_{w}(y_{i}),\gr_{z}(y_{i})\right)=\left(\frac{1}{2}-2n+i,\frac{1}{2}-i\right).

If
i=0
or
i=2n
, then the same formula holds for
y_{i}
.

Proof.

Recall that

\partial
has
(\gr_{w},\gr_{z})
-bigrading of
(-1,-1)
, and that
\mathscr{U}
and
\mathscr{V}
have bigradings
(-2,0)
and
(0,-2)
, respectively. Using the description in Figure
5
, it is easy to check that the formula holds up to an overall additive constant. That is, the formula holds for the relative
\gr_{w}
and
\gr_{z}
gradings. Hence, it is sufficient to show that the absolute
\gr_{w}
grading is correct for one of the generators, and similarly for the
\gr_{z}
grading. To check the absolute gradings, we note that if we set
\mathscr{V}=1
and
\mathscr{U}=0
, then we recover the Heegaard Floer complex for
\widehat{\CF}(S^{3},w_{1},w_{2})
, which is homotopy equivalent to
\mathbb{F}_{1/2}\oplus\mathbb{F}_{-1/2}
, as a
\gr_{w}
-graded chain complex. In this case, the complex has generators
y_{2n-1}
and
y_{2n}
, which pins down their
\gr_{w}
-grading. A similar argument computes the
\gr_{z}
-gradings.
user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

We now compute the homology action
A_{\gamma}
on the complex of the knotification of
T(2,2n)
. In order to use Proposition
2.10
, we need to compute
A_{\lambda}
and
\Phi_{w_{2}}
. For a choice of arc on the Heegaard surface as in Figure
4
, by counting bigons we obtain that
A_{\lambda}
has the form

(4.20)

\begin{split}A_{\lambda}(x_{0})&=\mathscr{U}(x_{1}+x_{4n-1}),\\
A_{\lambda}(x_{2n})&=\mathscr{V}(x_{2n-1}+x_{2n+1})\\
A_{\lambda}(x_{i})&=\mathscr{U}x_{4n-i-1}\\
A_{\lambda}(x_{i})&=\mathscr{U}x_{4n-i+1}\end{split}\quad\begin{split}\,&\\
\,&\\
&\text{if }0<i<2n,\quad\text{and}\\
&\text{if }2n+1<i<4n.\end{split}

Next, we need to understand the map
\Phi_{w_{2}}
. Counting bigons on diagrams like those shown in Figure
4
implies that
\Phi_{w_{2}}
takes the following form:

(4.21)

\begin{split}\Phi_{w_{2}}(x_{2i})&=x_{2i+1}\\
\Phi_{w_{2}}(x_{2i+1})&=x_{2i}+x_{4n-2i}\\
\Phi_{w_{2}}(x_{2i})&=x_{4n-2i+1}\\
\Phi_{w_{2}}(x_{2n+1})&=x_{2n},\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}&\text{if }0<i<n,\\
&\text{if }n<i<2n,\\
&\text{if }n<i<2n,\\
&\,\end{split}

and
\Phi_{w_{2}}
vanishes on all other generators.

Finally, we combine Proposition
2.10
with (
4.20
) and (
4.21
) to obtain the following formula for
A_{\gamma}\simeq A_{\lambda}+\mathscr{U}\Phi_{w_{2}}
on the knotified complex, which we write in terms of the basis from (
4.17
):

(4.22)

\begin{split}A_{\gamma}(x_{2i+1})&=\mathscr{U}y_{2i+2}+\mathscr{U}x_{2i+2}\\
A_{\gamma}(x_{2i})&=\mathscr{U}y_{2i+1}\\
A_{\gamma}(y_{2i})&=\mathscr{U}y_{2i+1}\\
A_{\gamma}(y_{2n})&=\mathscr{V}y_{2n-1},\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}&\text{ if }0\leq i<n-1,\\
&\text{ if }0<i<n-1\\
&\text{ if }0\leq i<n,\end{split}

and
A_{\gamma}
vanishes on all other generators. The example of
T(2,6)
is shown below:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The dashed lines denote the differential and the solid lines denote the
A_{\gamma}
action.
It is convenient to modify the map
A_{\gamma}
by a further chain homotopy, so that it takes one staircase summand to the other, with no self arrows, as follows.

Define a function
\delta\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{F}
by the formula

\delta(n)=n(n-1)/2\mod 2.

Conceptually, it is easier to think of
\delta(n)
as the sequence
0,0,1,1,0,0,1,1,\dots
. We define a homotopy
F
as follows. On the first staircase summand, we define
F
via the formula

\begin{split}F(y_{2i})&=\delta(2i)\cdot y_{2i}\\
F(x_{2i+1})&=\delta(2i+1)\cdot x_{2i+1}\end{split}\quad\begin{split}&\text{if }\quad 0\leq i\leq n,\\
&\text{if }\quad 0\leq i<n.\end{split}

On the second staircase summand, we define
F
via the formula

\begin{split}F(y_{2i+1})=\delta(2i)\cdot y_{2i+1}\\
F(x_{2i})=\delta(2i-1)\cdot x_{2i}\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}&\text{ if }\quad 0\leq i<n\\
&\text{ if }\quad 0<i<n.\end{split}

Writing
A^{\prime}_{\gamma}
for
A_{\gamma}+[\partial,F]
, we compute that

\begin{split}A_{\gamma}^{\prime}(y_{2i})&=\mathscr{U}y_{2i+1}\\
A_{\gamma}^{\prime}(x_{2i+1})&=\mathscr{U}x_{2i+2}\\
A_{\gamma^{\prime}}(y_{2n})&=\mathscr{V}y_{2n-1}.\end{split}\qquad\begin{split}&\text{ if }\quad 0\leq i<n,\\
&\text{ if }\quad 0\leq i<n-1,\\
&\,\end{split}

Continuing our running example of
T(2,6)
, equation (
4.23
) becomes the following

(4.24)
