We summarize the above computation as follows:

Proposition 4.25
.

The pair
(\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(S^{2}\times S^{1},H_{n}),A_{\gamma})
has a model where
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(S^{2}\times S^{1},H_{n})
is equal to
\mathcal{S}^{n}\{\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\}\oplus\mathcal{S}^{n-1}\{-\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}\}
and
A_{\gamma}
maps
\mathcal{S}^{n}
to
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
on the chain level. Here, we recall that
\{i,j\}
denotes a shift in the
(\gr_{w},\gr_{z})
-grading by
(i,j)
, and
\mathcal{S}^{n}
and
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
are the chain complexes in Definition
4.7
.

We now consider mirror of the torus link
T(2,2n)
, which we denote by
\underline{L}_{n}
. We denote its knotification by
\underline{H}_{n}
. On the level of Floer complexes, taking the mirror amounts to replacing the link Floer complex by the dual complex over the ring
\mathscr{R}^{-}
. In practice, this amounts to reversing all the arrows in the differential and multiplying the
(\gr_{w},\gr_{z})
-bigrading by an overall factor of
-1
. The homology action on the mirror is also the dual. We summarize this as follows:

Proposition 4.26
.

The pair
(\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(S^{2}\times S^{1},\underline{H}_{n}),A_{\gamma})
has a model where
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(S^{2}\times S^{1},\underline{H}_{n})
is equal to
\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{n}\{-\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}\}\oplus\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{n-1}\{\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\}
and
A_{\gamma}
maps
\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{n-1}
to
\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{n}
on the chain level.

4.4.
The Borromean knot
{\mathcal{B}_{0}}

Let
{\mathcal{B}_{0}}\subset\#^{2}S^{2}\times S^{1}
be the Borromean knot,
that is, the knot
obtained from the Borromean rings by a zero-framed surgery on two of its components.
The Heegaard Floer chain complex of
{\mathcal{B}_{0}}
is described in
[
38
, Proposition 9.2]
.
We adapt the calculation of
[
4
, Section 5]
and

[
1
, Section 4]
to the present context.

The chain complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}({\mathcal{B}_{0}})
is homotopy equivalent to
\mathbb{F}^{4}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathscr{R}^{-}
, with vanishing differential. We write
1,x,y,xy
for the generators of
\mathbb{F}^{4}
, which we can think of as being generators of
H^{*}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. The bigradings are as follows:

(4.27)

\begin{split}(\gr_{w}(1),\gr_{z}(1))=(1,-1),\quad&(\gr_{w}(x),\gr_{z}(x))=(\gr_{w}(y),\gr_{z}(y))=(0,0),\quad\text{and}\\
&(\gr_{w}(xy),\gr_{z}(xy))=(-1,1).\end{split}

Up to an overall grading preserving isomorphism, the
H_{1}(\#^{2}S^{2}\times S^{1})
module structure is uniquely determined by the formal properties of the action. In detail, if we write
x^{*}
and
y^{*}
for the two generators of
H_{1}(\#^{2}S^{2}\times S^{1})
, then the module structure takes the following form (up to overall isomorphism):

For the explicit description of the top and bottom towers of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}({\mathcal{B}_{0}})
, we refer the readers to
[
4
, Section 5]
.

5.
On tensor products of staircase complexes

In this section, we compute the correction terms of certain tensor products of staircase complexes.

5.1.
Staircase complexes

A
positive staircase complex

\mathcal{C}
is a bigraded chain complex over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
with generators

x_{0},y_{1},x_{2},\dots,y_{2n-1},x_{2n}
for some
n>0
with differential given by

\partial y_{2i+1}=\mathscr{U}^{\alpha_{i}}\cdot x_{2i}+\mathscr{V}^{\beta_{i}}\cdot x_{2i+2}

extended equivariantly over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
, for some positive integers
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
. We assume that
\partial
,
\mathscr{U}
and
\mathscr{V}
are
(-1,-1)
,
(-2,0)
and
(0,-2)
bigraded, respectively. We assume that
\alpha_{i}=\beta_{n-i-1}
. Furthermore, we assume the gradings are normalized so that
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}/(\mathscr{U}-1))\cong\mathbb{F}[\mathscr{V}]
has generator with
\gr_{z}
-grading
0
, and
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}/(\mathscr{V}-1))\cong\mathbb{F}[\mathscr{U}]
has generator with
\gr_{w}
-grading
0
. A
negative staircase complex
is the dual complex of a positive staircase complex.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}=(P_{1}\to P_{0})
is a positive staircase complex, which we view as a free complex over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
.

(1)

H_{*}(\mathcal{P})
is torsion free as an
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-module.

(2)

There is a
(\gr_{w},\gr_{z})
-grading preserving chain map

F\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathscr{R}^{-},

which sends
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycles to
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycles. Furthermore
F
may be taken to map each generator of
P_{0}
to a non-zero monomial in
\mathscr{R}^{-}
, and vanish on
P_{1}
.

Proof.

For the first claim, using the grading properties of
\mathcal{P}
it is sufficient to show that
\mathscr{U}^{i}\mathscr{V}^{j}\cdot[x]\neq 0
if
[x]\neq 0\in H_{*}(\mathcal{P})
. To see this, it is sufficient to see that if
x\in P_{0}
and
\mathscr{U}^{i}\mathscr{V}^{j}\cdot x\in\im(P_{1})
, then
x\in\im(P_{1})
. This is straightforward to verify.

For the second claim, we note that the stated map
F
is clearly a chain map and sends
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycles to
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cyles.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Definition 5.2
.

We call a complex
\mathcal{C}
a
positive multi-staircase
if it is the tensor product of a nonzero number of positive staircase complexes. We call
\mathcal{C}
a
negative multi-staircase
if it is the tensor product of a nonzero number of negative staircases.

The dual of a positive multi-staircase is a negative multi-staircase, and vice-versa.

By construction, a positive staircase
\mathcal{C}
has a
\mathbb{Z}
-filtration with two levels, and we write
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1}\to C_{0})
. Hence, a positive multi-staircase with
n
factors has a
\mathbb{Z}
-filtration with
n+1
non-trivial levels, for which we denote

(5.3)

\mathcal{C}=(C_{n}\to C_{n-1}\to\cdots\to C_{1}\to C_{0}).

In general, the sequence in equation (
5.3
) will not be exact. As an example, one may consider the Floer homology of
T(2,3)\#T(2,3)\#T(2,3)
. If
\mathcal{C}=(C_{n}\to\cdots\to C_{0})
is a positive multi-staircase, we say that
\mathcal{C}
is an
exact
multi-staircase if the following sequence is exact:

0\to C_{n}\to\cdots\to C_{0}.

In particular, an exact multi-staircase is a free resolution of its homology.

Lemma 5.4
.

(1)

Every positive staircase is exact.

(2)

The tensor product of two positive staircases is exact.

Proof.

Exactness of a positive staircase
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1}\to C_{0})
amounts to the claim that the map
C_{1}\to C_{0}
is injective, which is easy to verify.

Next, suppose
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1}\to C_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1}\to D_{0})
are staircases. We claim that their tensor product is also exact. Let
\mathcal{E}=(E_{2}\to E_{1}\to E_{0})
denote this tensor product. Clearly the map
E_{2}\to E_{1}
is injective, so it is sufficient to show that
H_{*}(E_{1})=0
. The homology
H_{*}(\mathcal{E})
decomposes as the direct sum
H_{*}(E_{2})\oplus H_{*}(E_{1})\oplus H_{*}(E_{0})
. Since every
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion element contains a non-zero summand of
H_{*}(E_{0})
, it follows that
H_{*}(E_{1})
consists only of
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-torsion elements. Since
\mathcal{E}
is bigraded, it follows that each element
[x]\in H_{*}(E_{1})
satisfies
\mathscr{U}^{j}\mathscr{V}^{k}\cdot[x]=0
for some
j
and
k
. In particular, if
x\in E_{1}
is a cycle, then
\mathscr{U}^{j}\mathscr{V}^{k}\cdot x\in\im(E_{2}\to E_{1})
. In particular, to show that
H_{*}(E_{1})=0
it is sufficient to show that
\im(E_{2}\to E_{1})
contains no elements of the form
\mathscr{U}^{i}\mathscr{V}^{j}\cdot x
with
i+j>0
. To see this, note that the map from
E_{2}
is the sum of the maps
C_{1}\otimes D_{1}\to C_{1}\otimes D_{0}
and
C_{1}\otimes D_{1}\to C_{0}\otimes D_{1}
. Neither of these maps has image containing any elements of the form
\mathscr{U}^{i}\mathscr{V}^{j}\cdot x
for
i+j>0
, since positive staircase complexes have torsion free homology.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

5.2.
V_{s}
-invariants of tensor products of staircases

In this subsection,
we compute the
V_{s}
-invariants of certain tensor products of staircases. We wish to understand the
V_{s}
-invariants of tensor products of staircases where some factors are positive and some negative. Of course, we may group factors and write such a complex as a tensor product of
\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P}
, where
\mathcal{N}
is a negative multi-staircase, and
\mathcal{P}
is a positive multi-staircase. Clearly,

\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P}\cong\Mor_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P}),

where
\Mor_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(N^{\vee},\mathcal{P})
denotes the chain complex of
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-module homomorphisms from
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
to
\mathcal{P}
. In particular, to understand the
V_{s}
-invariants of arbitrary tensor products of positive and negative staircases, it is sufficient to understand the morphism complex between two positive multi-staircases.

It is also helpful to note that if
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{P}
are multi-staircases (of either sign), then a cycle
\phi\in\Mor_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P})
is
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion as a morphism if and only if
\phi
maps
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycles to
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycles.

The following result is by now classical. (See
[
5
, Proposition 5.1]
).

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=(P_{n}\to\cdots\to P_{0})
be a positive multi-staircase and let
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then

V_{s}(\mathcal{P})=\min_{x\in\mathcal{G}(P_{0})}\max(\alpha(x),\beta(x)-s),

where
\alpha(x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\gr_{w}(x)
,
\beta(x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\gr_{z}(x)
, and
\mathcal{G}(P_{0})
denotes the set of homogeneously graded generators of
P_{0}
.

Proof.

Lemma
5.1
implies that a homogenously graded element
x\in\mathcal{P}
is an
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycle if and only if an odd number of elements
\mathcal{G}(P_{0})
are represented as non-trivial summands. In particular, the individual elements of
\mathcal{G}(P_{0})
determine the correction terms
V_{s}
. The expression
-2\max(\alpha(x),\beta(x)-s)
is the maximal
\gr_{w}
-grading of an element of the form
\mathscr{U}^{m}\mathscr{V}^{n}x
such that
m,n\geq 0
and
x\in\mathscr{A}_{s}
. Taking the minimum over all
x\in\mathcal{G}(P_{0})
gives the result.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

We now pass to studying
V_{s}
invariants of products of positive and negative multi-staircases. We begin with the following statement.

Proposition 5.6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}=(P_{m}\to\cdots\to P_{0})
and
\mathcal{Q}=(Q_{n}\to\cdots\to Q_{0})
are two positive multi-staircases.

(1)

In general,

V_{s}(\Mor(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))\geq V_{s}(\Hom_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(H_{*}(\mathcal{P}),H_{*}(\mathcal{Q}))=V_{s}(\Hom(P_{0}/\im(P_{1}),Q_{0}/\im(Q_{1}))).

(2)

If
\mathcal{Q}
is exact, then
V_{s}(\Mor(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}))=V_{s}(\Hom_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(H_{*}(\mathcal{P}),H_{*}(\mathcal{Q})).

Proof.

The inequality of part (
1
) follows since there is a grading preserving map of
\mathscr{R}^{-}
modules

H_{*}\Mor_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\to\Hom_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(P_{0}/\im(P_{1}),Q_{0}/\im(Q_{1})),

which sends
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion elements to
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion elements. The equality in part (
1
) follows since
H_{*}(\mathcal{P})
decomposes as a direct sum

\bigoplus_{s=0}^{n}\big(\ker(P_{i}\to P_{i-1})/\im(P_{i+1}\to P_{i})\big),

and
P_{0}/\im(P_{1}\to P_{0})
is the only summand which contains
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion elements.

We now consider the second claim. Suppose that
\mathcal{Q}
is exact. We will show

(5.7)

V_{s}(\Hom(P_{0}/\im(P_{1}),Q_{0}/\im(Q_{1})))\geq V_{s}(\Mor(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})).

Suppose
\phi\colon P_{0}/\im(P_{1})\to Q_{0}/\im(Q_{1})
is an
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-module homomorphism which maps
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion elements to
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion elements. It suffices to extend
\phi
to obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}/\im(P_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}/\im(Q_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

The construction of the maps
\phi_{i}
follows from the same procedure as in
[
50
, Theorem 2.2.6 and the discussion below it]
. We briefly summarize the construction. The map
\phi_{0}
may be chosen since
P_{0}
is free, and hence projective, and
Q_{0}\to Q_{0}/\im(Q_{1})
is surjective. Having constructed
\phi_{0}
, we next construct
\phi_{1}
. Using exactness of
\mathcal{Q}
, we may factor
\phi_{0}\circ(P_{1}\to P_{0})
into
\im(Q_{1}\to Q_{0})
. Using the fact that
P_{1}
is projective and
Q_{1}\to\im(Q_{1}\to Q_{0})
is surjective, we obtain a map
\phi_{1}
. We repeat this process until we exhaust
\mathcal{P}
. This gives (
5.7
), completing the proof.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{N}=(N_{0}\to\cdots\to N_{n})
is a negative multi-staircase, and
\mathcal{P}=(P_{m}\to\cdots\to P_{0})
is a positive multi-staircase. Write
\mathcal{G}(P_{i})
for the generators of
P_{i}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{G}(N_{i})
.

(1)

In general

(5.9)

V_{s}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P})\geq-\frac{1}{2}\min_{x\in\mathcal{G}(N_{0})}\max_{y\in\mathcal{G}(P_{0})}\min\big(\gr_{w}(x)+\gr_{w}(y),\gr_{z}(x)+\gr_{z}(y)+2s\big).

(2)

If
\mathcal{P}=(P_{1}\to P_{0})
is a positive staircase, then (
5.9
) is an equality.

Proof.

We dualize, and consider the isomorphism
\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P}\cong\Mor(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P})
. For the first claim, suppose
\phi\in\Mor(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P})
is an
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycle which is of homogeneous grading
(d,d-2s)
, where
d=d(\mathscr{A}_{s}(\Mor(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P}))
. Note
\phi\in\mathscr{A}_{s}(\Mor(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P}))
. For each
x^{\vee}\in\mathcal{G}(N_{0}^{\vee})
,
\phi(x^{\vee})
is a
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-non-torsion cycle, and hence must contain a summand of the form
f\cdot y
, for some non-zero monomial
f\in\mathscr{R}^{-}
and
y\in\mathcal{G}(P_{0})
. By the definition of the grading of a morphism, we have

\gr_{w}(y)-\gr_{w}(x^{\vee})+\gr_{w}(f)=d\quad\text{and}\quad\gr_{z}(y)-\gr_{z}(x^{\vee})+\gr_{z}(f)=d-2s.

Since
\gr_{w}(f)\leq 0
and
\gr_{z}(f)\leq 0
, and
(\gr_{w}(x^{\vee}),\gr_{z}(x^{\vee})=(-\gr_{w}(x),-\gr_{z}(x))
, we have that for each
x

d(\mathscr{A}_{s}(\Mor(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P}))\leq\max_{y\in\mathcal{G}(P_{0})}\min(\gr_{w}(x)+\gr_{w}(y),\gr_{z}(x)+\gr_{z}(y)+2s).

Taking the minimum over
x\in\mathcal{G}(N_{0})
gives the statement.

We now consider the second claim. Suppose that
\mathcal{P}=(P_{1}\to P_{0})
is a positive staircase.
Using Lemma
5.4
and Proposition
5.6
, we know that

V_{s}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P})=V_{s}(\Hom_{\mathscr{R}^{-}}(N_{0}^{\vee}/\im(N_{1}^{\vee}),P_{0}/\im(P_{1}))).

Fix
s\geq 0
. Let
\delta_{s}
denote the right-hand side of (
5.9
), without the factor of
-1/2
. For each
x^{\vee}
in
\mathcal{G}(N_{0}^{\vee})
, we pick a
y_{x}\in\mathcal{G}(P_{0})
so that

\gr_{w}(y_{x})-\gr_{w}(x^{\vee})\geq d\quad\text{and}\quad\gr_{z}(y_{x})-\gr_{z}(x^{\vee})\geq d-2s.

We set
\phi_{0}\colon N_{0}^{\vee}\to P_{0}
to be the map which takes
x^{\vee}
to
f_{x}\cdot y_{x}
, where
f_{x}\in\mathscr{R}^{-}
is the unique monomial so that
\phi_{0}
has bigrading
(d,d-2s)
. By composition, we obtain a map
\phi^{\prime}\colon N_{0}^{\vee}\to P_{0}/\im(P_{1})
.

Claim.
The map
\phi^{\prime}
vanishes on
\im(N_{1}^{\vee})
.

Given the claim, we quickly conclude the proof. In fact, we obtain a map
\phi
from
N_{0}/\im(N_{1})
to
P_{1}/\im(P_{0})
. Hence, we may use the second part of Proposition
5.6
to conclude that

d(\mathscr{A}_{s}(\Mor(\mathcal{N}^{\vee},\mathcal{P})))\geq\delta_{s},

which completes the proof modulo the claim.

It remains to prove the claim. Let
y_{1}\in N_{1}^{\vee}
. We consider the element
v=\partial(y_{1})\in N_{0}^{\vee}
. We can write
v
as a sum
\sum_{x^{\vee}\in\mathcal{G}(N_{0}^{\vee})}f_{x}\cdot x^{\vee}
, where each
f_{x}
is a monomial. Tensoring the maps from the second part of Lemma
5.1
, we obtain a chain map from
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
to
\mathscr{R}^{-}
, which is non-zero only on
N_{0}^{\vee}
, and furthermore maps each generator of
N_{0}^{\vee}
to a monomial. Using the fact that this map is a chain map, we see that the number of
x^{\vee}\in\mathcal{G}(N_{0}^{\vee})
where
f_{x}
is non-zero is even. It follows immediately that
\phi_{0}(v)
is an
\mathscr{R}^{-}
-torsion cycle. By Lemma
5.1
,
H_{*}(\mathcal{P})
is torsion free, so it follows that
[\phi_{0}(v)]=0\in H_{*}(\mathcal{P})=P_{0}/\im(P_{1})
. This proves the claim and completes the proof of Proposition
5.8
.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

5.3.
A counterexample

We give an example indictating that the second statement of Proposition
5.8
need not hold if
\mathcal{P}
is a product of more than one positive staircase, even if
\mathcal{P}
is exact.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{1}
,
\mathcal{P}^{2}
be the staircases of torus knots
T(6,7)
and
T(4,5)
, respectively. As described in Subsection
4.1
,
the generators of
\mathcal{P}^{1}
are at bigradings
(-30,0)
,
(-30,-2)
,
(-20,-2)
,
(-20,-6)
,
(-12,-6)
,
(-12,-12)
,
(-6,-12)
,
(-6,-20)
,
(-2,-20)
,
(-2,-30)
,
(0,-30)
.
We denote these generators by
a_{0},\dots,a_{10}
. We have
\partial a_{2i}=0
and
\partial a_{2i+1}=\mathscr{U}^{\alpha_{i}}a_{2i+2}+\mathscr{V}^{\beta_{i}}a_{2i}
,
where
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
are non-negative integers determined by the condition that
\partial
preserve the grading. In particular, the generators
with odd index generate
\mathcal{P}^{1}_{1}
, while the generators with even index span
\mathcal{P}^{1}_{0}
.

Likewise, there are generators
b_{0},\dots,b_{6}
for
\mathcal{P}^{2}
with bigradings
(-12,0)
,
(-12,-2)
,
(-6,-2)
,
(-6,-6)
,
(-2,-6)
,
(-2,-12)
,
(0,-12)
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{2}
. The only elements
x
in
\mathcal{P}
such that
\gr_{w}(x)=\gr_{z}(x)>-18
are linear combinations of

\mathscr{U}^{i}\mathscr{V}^{j}a_{4}\otimes b_{4}
with
(i,j)=(0,1),(1,2)
and
\mathscr{U}^{i^{\prime}}\mathscr{V}^{j^{\prime}}a_{6}\otimes b_{2}
with
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(1,0),(2,1)
.

Proof.

Direct inspection.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Let now
\mathcal{N}
be the negative staircase complex of the mirror of the trefoil. It is generated by elements
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}

at bigradings
(2,0),(2,2),(0,2)
, respectively. The differential is
\partial c_{0}=\mathscr{V}c_{1}
,
\partial c_{2}=\mathscr{U}c_{1}
,
\partial c_{1}=0
. That is,

c_{0},c_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
,
c_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{-1}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

There is no cycle
z\in\mathscr{A}_{0}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P})
such that
\gr_{w}(z)\geq-12
and
z\neq 0
.

Proof.

Any such cycle would be a linear combination of elements of type
\mathscr{U}^{i}\mathscr{V}^{j}\cdot a_{k}\otimes b_{\ell}\otimes c_{m}
. By Lemma
5.10
,
unless
(k,\ell)=(4,4)
or
(6,2)
, the
\gr_{w}
-grading of such combination is at most
-14
. Hence,
if
z\in\mathscr{A}_{0}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P})
and
z\neq 0
has
\gr_{w}(z)\geq-12
, then
z
has to be a linear combination of elements of the two-element set

a_{4}\otimes b_{4}\otimes c_{0},a_{6}\otimes b_{2}\otimes c_{2}.

But then,
z
is not a cycle.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Corollary 5.12
.

We have
V_{0}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{P})\geq 7
.

The following result shows that the right-hand side of
(
5.9
) is strictly smaller than 7.

Lemma 5.13
.

The expression

-\frac{1}{2}\min_{x\in G(\mathcal{N}_{0})}\max_{y\in G(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\min(\gr_{w}(x)+\gr_{w}(y),\gr_{z}(x)+\gr_{z}(y))

is equal to
6
.

Proof.

For
x=c_{0}
, the expression

(5.14)

\max_{y\in G(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\min(\gr_{w}(x)+\gr_{w}(y),\gr_{z}(x)+\gr_{z}(y))

is equal to
-12
with the equality attained at
y=a_{4}\otimes b_{4}
. For
x=c_{2}
, (
5.14
)
attains its maximal value
-12
for
y=a_{6}\otimes b_{2}
.

/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

5.4.
More on the
V_{s}
-invariants of tensor products of staircases

In this subsection, we highlight some special cases of Proposition
5.5
and Proposition
5.8
which will be useful for our purposes.

Corollary 5.15
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a positive multi-staircase, and for
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
, let
\mathcal{S}^{n_{i}}
denote the staircase complex of
Definition
4.7
with
\sum n_{i}\geq 0
. Then

V_{s}(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n_{r}})=\min_{0\leq j\leq\sum n_{r}}\left(V_{s+2j-\sum n_{i}}(\mathcal{C})+j\right).

Proof.

By Proposition
4.9
, we know that
\mathcal{S}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n_{r}}
is locally equivalent to
\mathcal{S}^{n}
, where
n=\sum n_{i}
, so by Proposition
2.29
it suffices to prove the result when
i=1
. Write
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
for the generators of
C_{0}
, and write
y_{0},\dots,y_{n}
for the generators of
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{0}
. Then
x_{i}\otimes y_{j}
forms a basis of homogeneously graded elements of
(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n})_{0}
. By Proposition
5.5
, we have

V_{s}(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
0\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}\max(\alpha(x_{i})+\alpha(y_{j}),\beta(x_{i})+\beta(x_{j})-s).

We note that
\alpha(y_{j})=j
and
\beta(y_{j})=n-j
, so we conclude that

\begin{split}V_{s}(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n})&=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
0\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}\max(\alpha(x_{i})+j,\beta(x_{i})+n-j-s)\\
&=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\min_{1\leq i\leq m}\left(\max(\alpha(x_{i}),\beta(x_{i})+n-2j-s)+j\right)\\
&=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\left(V_{s+2j-n}(\mathcal{C})+j\right),\end{split}

completing the proof.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

We have the following corollary of Proposition
5.8
:

Corollary 5.16
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a positive staircase, and for
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
, let
\mathcal{S}^{n_{i}}
denote the staircase complexes of
Definition
4.7
. Assume
\sum n_{i}<0
. Then

V_{s}(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n_{r}})=\max_{0\leq j\leq n}\left(V_{s-2j+n}(\mathcal{C})-j\right),

where
n=-\sum n_{i}
.

Remark 5.17
.

In contrast to Corollary
5.15
, where
\mathcal{C}
was allowed to be a positive
multi-
staircase (i.e., a tensor product of positive staircases), in Corollary
5.16

we require that
\mathcal{C}
be a positive
staircase
.

Proof of Corollary
5.16
:

As in the proof of Corollary
5.15
,
\mathcal{S}^{n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{S}^{n_{r}}
is locally equivalent to
\mathcal{S}^{-n}
, so it is sufficient to consider the case when
i=1
.
Write
x_{1},\dots,x_{q}
for the generators of
C_{0}
, and
y_{0},\dots,y_{n}
for the generators of the 0-level of
\mathcal{S}^{-n}
.
According to Proposition
5.8
:

(5.18)

\begin{split}V_{s}(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{-n})&=\max_{0\leq i\leq n}\min_{1\leq j\leq q}\max(\alpha(x_{j})+\alpha(y_{i}),\beta(x_{j})+\beta(y_{i})-s)\\
&=\max_{0\leq i\leq n}\min_{1\leq j\leq q}\max(\alpha(x_{j})-i,\beta(x_{j})-n+i-s)\\
&=\max_{0\leq i\leq n}\min_{1\leq j\leq q}\left(\max(\alpha(x_{j}),\beta(x_{j})-n+2i-s)-i\right)\\
&=\max_{0\leq i\leq n}\left(V_{s-2i+n}(\mathcal{C})-i\right).\end{split}

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

5.5.
Knots with split towers

We now introduce the notion of a knot complex with
split towers
. The correction terms of a knot complex with split towers have a relatively simple form. An important example of a knot with split towers are connected sums of knotifications of positive and negative
T(2,2n)
torus
links.

Definition 5.19

(Split towers)
.

Let
K
be a knot in
Y=\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1}
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a chain complex which is free and finitely generated over
\mathscr{R}^{-}
and is homotopy equivalent to
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(Y,K,\mathfrak{s}_{0})
. We say that
\mathcal{C}
has

split towers
if there exists a basis
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m}

of
H_{1}(\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z})
and subcomplexes
\mathcal{C}^{I}_{*}\subset\mathcal{C}
, indexed over subsets
I\subset\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m}\}
, such that the following are satisfied:

(a)

\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{I\subset\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m}\}}\mathcal{C}^{I}
;

(b)

If
\gamma_{i}\not\in I
, then
A_{\gamma_{i}}
takes
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{I})
to
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{I\cup\{\gamma_{i}\}})
, and becomes an isomorphism after inverting
\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V}
. If
\gamma_{i}\in I
, then
A_{\gamma_{i}}
vanishes on
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{I})
, after inverting
\mathscr{U},\mathscr{V}
.

Abusing notation slightly, we say a knot
K
has split towers if there is a representative of
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(Y,K)
which has split towers. Note that in many of our examples, the homology action actually respects the splitting on the chain level, i.e.
A_{\gamma_{i}}
maps
\mathcal{C}^{I}
to
\mathcal{C}^{I\cup\{\gamma_{i}\}}
if
\gamma_{i}\not\in I
, and
A_{\gamma_{i}}
vanishes on
\mathcal{C}^{I}
if
\gamma_{i}\in I
.

Example 5.20
.

•

Any knot
K
in
S^{3}
has split towers (trivially).

•

The knotification of the
T(2,2n)
torus link has split towers. See Proposition
4.25
.

•

The Borromean knot does not have split towers.

Lemma 5.21
.

If
K
and
K^{\prime}
have split towers, then
K\#K^{\prime}
has split towers.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the Künneth formula.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Proposition 5.22
.

Suppose
K
is a knot in
\#^{m}S^{2}\times S^{1}
with split towers. Write

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}=\mathcal{C}^{\emptyset}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}=\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m}}.

Then

V_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}(K)=V_{s}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}})\quad\text{and}\quad V_{s}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K)=V_{s}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}).

Suppose, additionally, that
n>0
and
{\mathcal{B}_{0}}
is the Borromean knot. Then

\displaystyle V_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}(K\#^{n}{\mathcal{B}_{0}})=

\displaystyle-\frac{n}{2}+\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\left(V_{s+2j-n}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}})+j\right)

\displaystyle V_{s}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K\#^{n}{\mathcal{B}_{0}})=

\displaystyle-\frac{n}{2}+\max_{0\leq j\leq n}\left(V_{s+2j-n}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}})+j\right).

Proof.

We consider first the proof that
V_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}(K)=V_{s}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}})
. It is sufficient to show that

(5.23)

d^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K))=d(\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}),

where
\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{top}}
denotes the subcomplex of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}
in Alexander grading
s
, and both
d
invariants are computed with respect to the
\gr_{w}
-grading. By definition,
d^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K))
is the maximal grading of a homogeneously graded element of
H_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K))
which maps to an element of
U^{-1}H_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K))
having non-trivial image in
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{top}}
; see Subsection
2.6
.
Since
K
has split towers, the cokernel above is spanned by
U^{-1}H_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}_{s})
, and
H_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{I}_{s})
has trivial image for
I\neq\emptyset
, equation (
5.23
) follows.

The claim about
d^{\mathrm{bot}}
is similar. In this case,
d^{\mathrm{bot}}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K))
is defined as the maximal grading of a homogeneous element in
H_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K))/\Tors
which is in the image of
\mathcal{H}^{bot}
. This is clearly
d(\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{bot}})
.

We pass now to the second part of the proof. An analogous argument appeared in
[
1
,
4
]
; we recall it for completeness.
The complex
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}({\mathcal{B}_{0}})
is described in Section
4.4
. Since
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}({\mathcal{B}_{0}})
has vanishing differential, we obtain

H_{*}(\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(K)\otimes\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}({\mathcal{B}_{0}})^{\otimes n})\cong\mathcal{H\!F\!K}^{-}(K)\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{B}^{\otimes n},

where
\mathbb{B}
is the 4-dimensional vector space spanned by
1
,
x
,
y
and
xy
, whose bigradings are shown in equation (
4.27
).

We first consider the claim about
V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}
. Using the
H_{1}
-action on
\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}({\mathcal{B}_{0}})
described in Section
4.4
, one easily obtains the following:
a cycle
x\in\mathscr{A}_{s}(K\#^{n}{\mathcal{B}_{0}})
is of homogeneous
\gr_{w}
-grading
d
, is
\mathbb{F}[U]
-non-torsion, and maps to the kernel of the
H_{1}
action in
U^{-1}H_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K\#B^{\#n}))
if and only if it has the form

\sum_{\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}x_{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}\otimes\epsilon_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\epsilon_{a_{n}},

where
\epsilon_{-1}=1\in\mathbb{B}
and
\epsilon_{1}=xy\in\mathbb{B}
with
\gr_{w}=1
and
-1
respectively. Moreover, each

x_{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}\in\mathcal{C}_{s+\sum a_{i}}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K)

is an
\mathbb{F}[U]
-non-torsion cycle of homogeneous
\gr_{w}
-grading
d+\sum a_{i}
. Noting that
\sum a_{i}
can be any integer of the form
n-2j
for
0\leq j\leq n
, we obtain that

d^{\mathrm{bot}}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K\#^{n}{\mathcal{B}_{0}}))=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\left(d(\mathcal{C}_{s+n-2j}^{\mathrm{bot}})-n+2j\right).

Multiplying by
-\tfrac{1}{2}
and switching
j
to
n-j
yields the statement.

The proof for
d^{\mathrm{top}}
is analogous. The cokernel of the
H_{1}
-action on
U^{-1}H_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K\#^{n}{\mathcal{B}_{0}}))
is spanned by any element of the form
x\otimes\epsilon_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\epsilon_{a_{n}}
where
\epsilon_{a_{i}}
are as above, and
x\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}_{s+\sum a_{i}}(K)
is a homogeneously graded,
\mathbb{F}[U]
-non-torsion element. Furthermore, any homogeneous element generating
U^{-1}H_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K\#^{n}{\mathcal{B}_{0}})
is a sum of an odd number of such elements. The same argument as before shows that

d^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathscr{A}_{s}(K\#^{n}{\mathcal{B}_{0}}))=\max_{0\leq j\leq n}\left(d(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}_{s+n-2j})-n+2j\right).

Multiplying by
-\tfrac{1}{2}
and switching
j
to
n-j
yields the statement.

user /Subtype /Link

\blacksquare

6.
Nonrational non-cuspidal complex curves

6.1.
General estimates

We now pass to main applications of our paper. Suppose
C\subset\mathbb{C}P^{2}
is a degree
d
curve. We mostly focus on the case when
C
is complex curve, but also consider the case where
C
is only a smooth surface, embedded away from a finite set of singular points, as in Definition
1.3
. We further assume that the singularities
of
C
are restricted to the following:

•

There are
\nu
cuspidal (unibranched) singular points
z_{1},\dots,z_{\nu}
. We write
K_{1},\dots,K_{\nu}
for their links, and set
K=K_{1}\#\cdots\#K_{\nu}
.

•

There are
m_{n}
singular points whose link is
T(2,2n)
.

•

There are
\underline{m}_{n}
singular points whose link is
-T(2,2n)
.

Define

\kappa_{+}=\sum_{n}nm_{n},\ \kappa_{-}=\sum_{n}n\underline{m}_{n},\ \eta_{+}=\sum_{n}m_{n},\ \eta_{-}=\sum_{n}\underline{m}_{n}.

Additionally, we assume that the curve has genus
g
given by the formula:

(6.1)

g=g(C)=\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}-g_{3}(K)-(\kappa_{+}+\kappa_{-})

For algebraic curves,
\kappa_{-}=0
and (
6.1
) is the adjunction formula. If
C
is a singular curve in the smooth category
of algebraic type (i.e.
\kappa_{-}=0
), the adjunction inequality implies that
g(C)
is greater or equal to the right-hand side
of (
6.1
). If
C
is of weak algebraic type, the relation between
g(C)
and the right-hand side of (
6.1
)
can be more involved.

We define

(6.2)

\begin{split}K_{+}&=K\#\hashsum\limits_{n}m_{n}H_{n}\\
\widetilde{K}&=K_{+}\#K_{-}\end{split}\qquad\qquad\qquad\begin{split}K_{-}&=\hashsum\limits_{n}\underline{m}_{n}\underline{H}_{n}\\
\widehat{K}&=\widetilde{K}\#_{g}{\mathcal{B}_{0}}\end{split}

where
H_{n}
denotes the knotification of the torus knot
T(2,2n)
, and
\underline{H}_{n}
denotes the knotification of its mirror.

Since the knots
K_{1},\dots,K_{\nu}
are algebraic knots, in particular, L-space knots, their knot Floer complexes are staircase complexes, which we denote by
\mathcal{C}(K_{i})
. In particular,

\mathcal{C\hskip-1.42262ptF\hskip-0.85358ptK}^{-}(K)=\mathcal{C}(K_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(K_{\nu})

is a positive multi-staircase complex.
Note that by Proposition
4.25
and Example
5.20
, the knots
K_{+}
,
K_{-}
, and
\widetilde{K}
have split towers. The following relations follow from Proposition
4.25
, the Künneth theorem for connected sums, and Proposition
4.9
. Here, we write
\cong
for homotopy equivalence of chain complexes, and
\underset{\mathrm{loc}}{\simeq}
for local equivalence.
The brackets denote an overall grading shift.

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}(K_{+})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}(K)\otimes\bigotimes_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{n})^{\otimes m_{n}}\{\tfrac{\eta_{+}}{2},\tfrac{\eta_{+}}{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K_{+})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K)\otimes\bigotimes_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{n-1})^{\otimes m_{n}}\{-\tfrac{\eta_{+}}{2},-\tfrac{\eta_{+}}{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}(K_{-})

\displaystyle\cong\bigotimes_{n}(\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{n-1})^{\otimes\underline{m}_{n}}\{\tfrac{\eta_{-}}{2},\tfrac{\eta_{-}}{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K_{-})

\displaystyle\cong\bigotimes_{n}(\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{n})^{\otimes\underline{m}_{n}}\{-\tfrac{\eta_{-}}{2},-\tfrac{\eta_{-}}{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}(\widetilde{K})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}(K_{+})\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}(K_{-})\underset{\mathrm{loc}}{\simeq}\mathcal{C}(K)\otimes\mathcal{S}^{\kappa_{+}-(\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-})}\{\tfrac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{2},\tfrac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}(\widetilde{K})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K_{+})\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}(K_{-})\underset{\mathrm{loc}}{\simeq}\mathcal{C}(K)\otimes\mathcal{S}^{\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}-\kappa_{-}}\{\tfrac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{2},\tfrac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{2}\}.

We set

\delta_{1}:=\kappa_{+}-(\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-}),\ \ \delta_{2}:=(\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+})-\kappa_{-}.

Whether the staircases in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{top}}({\widetilde{K}})
and
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bot}}(\widetilde{K})
are positive or negative depends on the signs of
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
. We gather now computations from Corollaries
5.15
and
5.16
, Proposition
4.6
, Proposition
4.26

as well Lemma
2.27
to obtain the following
result, which is the main tool towards Theorems
6.4
and
6.18
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose
K
,
\widetilde{K}
and
\widehat{K}
are as above.

(a)

If
\delta_{1}\geq 0
, then

\begin{split}V^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}(\widetilde{K})&=-\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\min_{0\leq j\leq\delta_{1}}(V_{s+2j-\delta_{1}}(K)+j)\\
V^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}(\widehat{K})&=-\frac{g}{2}-\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\min_{0\leq j\leq\delta_{1}+g}(V_{s+2j-\delta_{1}-g}(K)+j)\\
&=-\frac{g}{2}-\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\min_{0\leq j\leq\delta_{1}+g}(R(g_{3}(K)+s+2j-\delta_{1}-g)-(s+j-\delta_{1}-g)).\end{split}

(b)

If
\delta_{2}\geq 0
,
then

\begin{split}V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}(\widetilde{K})=&\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\min_{0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}}(V_{s+2j-\delta_{2}}(K)+j)\\
V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}(\widehat{K})=&\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}-\frac{g}{2}+\max_{0\leq i\leq g}\min_{0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}}(V_{s+2j+2i-g-\delta_{2}}(K)+i+j)\\
=&-\frac{g}{2}+\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}\\
&+\max_{0\leq i\leq g}\min_{0\leq j\leq\delta_{2}}(R(g_{3}(K)+s+2j+2i-g-\delta_{2})-(s+i+j-g-\delta_{2})).\end{split}

(c)

If
\delta_{1}<0
and
\mathcal{C}(K)
is a positive staircase
(not just a positive multi-staircase), then

\begin{split}V^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}(\widetilde{K})=&-\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\max_{0\leq j\leq-\delta_{1}}(V_{s-2j-\delta_{1}}(K)-j)\\
V^{\mathrm{top}}_{s}(\widehat{K})=&\frac{g}{2}-\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\min_{0\leq i\leq g}\max_{0\leq j\leq-\delta_{1}}(V_{s-2j-2i+g-\delta_{1}}(K)-i-j)\\
=&\frac{g}{2}-\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}\\
&+\min_{0\leq i\leq g}\max_{0\leq j\leq-\delta_{1}}(R(g_{3}(K)+s-2j-2i+g-\delta_{1})-(s-i-j+g-\delta_{1})).\end{split}

(d)

If
\delta_{2}<0
and
\mathcal{C}(K)
is a positive staircase,
then

\begin{split}V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}(\widetilde{K})&=\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\max_{0\leq j\leq-\delta_{2}}(V_{s-2j-\delta_{2}}(K)-j)\\
V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{s}(\widehat{K})&=\frac{g}{2}+\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\max_{0\leq j\leq g-\delta_{2}}(V_{s-2j+g-\delta_{2}}(K)-j)\\
&=\frac{g}{2}+\frac{\eta_{+}+\eta_{-}}{4}+\max_{0\leq j\leq g-\delta_{2}}(R(g_{3}(K)+s-2j+g-\delta_{2})-(s-j+g-\delta_{2})).\end{split}

Proposition
6.3
allows us to express the
d
-invariants of the boundary
Y=\partial N
of the tubular neighborhood
of
C
in terms of the
R
-functions of singular points. In our applications, we will focus on two cases.

(1)

Algebraic case.
We assume that
C
has only algebraic singularities, that is,
\underline{m}_{n}=0
for all
n>0
. This corresponds to items
(a) and (b) of Proposition
6.3
.

(2)

Single knot case.
We assume that
\nu=1
, so
K
is a positive staircase and
m_{n}=0
for all
n>0
. We will use items (c) and (d)
of Proposition
6.3
.

The first case is considered in Subsection
6.2
. The second is addressed in Subsection
6.4
.

6.2.
Curves with no negative double points

For the reader’s convenience we provide a full statement of the next result.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
C
be a reduced curve with degree
d
and genus
g
.
Suppose that
C
has cuspidal singular points
z_{1},\dots,z_{\nu}
, whose semigroup counting functions are
R_{1},\dots,R_{\nu}
, respectively.
Assume that apart from these
N
points, the curve
C
has, for each
n\geq 1
,
m_{n}\geq 0
singular points whose links are
T(2,2n)

(
A_{2n-1}
singular points
)
and no other singularities.
Define

\eta_{+}=\sum_{n}m_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa_{+}=\sum_{n}nm_{n}.

For any
k=1,\dots,d-2
, we have:

(6.5)

\begin{split}\max_{0\leq j\leq g}\min_{0\leq i\leq\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}}\left(R(kd+1-\eta_{+}-2i-2j)+i+j\right)&\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g\\
\min_{0\leq j\leq g+\kappa_{+}}\left(R(kd+1-2\gamma)+j\right)&\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}.\end{split}

Here
R
denotes the infimal convolution of the functions
R_{1},\dots,R_{\nu}
.

Proof.

Let
Y
be the boundary of a tubular neighborhood of
C
. Then
Y
is a result of a
d^{2}
surgery on

\widehat{K}\subset\#^{\rho}S^{2}\times S^{1}
obtained as in Subsection
6.2
, where
we readily compute from (
3.1
)
\rho=2g+\eta_{+}
.

Let
{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{j}
, for
j\in[-d^{2}/2,d^{2}/2)\cap\mathbb{Z}
denote the Spin
c
structures on
Y
as in Definition
2.32
.
By Lemma
3.12
,
{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{j}
extends to
\mathbb{C}P^{2}\setminus N
, if and only if
{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{j}
is a restriction of
{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{h}
for some
h
, where

{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{h}
is as in (
3.10
). By (
3.11
) we infer that this holds if and only if
j=md
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
if
d
is odd and

m\in\tfrac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
if
d
is even. Compare with
[
5
, Lemma 3.1]
.

By Proposition
3.13
, for any
md\in[-d^{2}/2,d^{2}/2)
such that
m+\frac{d-1}{2}
is an integer, we have

(6.6)

d_{\textrm{bot}}(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{md})\geq-\frac{\eta_{+}}{2}-g,\ \ d_{\textrm{top}}(Y,{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{md})\leq\frac{\eta_{+}}{2}+g.

By Theorem
2.34
, (
6.6
) translates to the inequalities

(6.7)

\begin{split}V^{\textrm{top}}_{md}(\widehat{K})&\geq\frac{(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1)}{8}-\frac{\eta_{+}}{4}-\frac{g}{2}\\
V^{\textrm{bot}}_{md}(\widehat{K})&\leq\frac{(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1)}{8}+\frac{\eta_{+}}{4}+\frac{g}{2}.\end{split}

We compute
V^{\mathrm{top}}_{md}
and
V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{md}
from
Proposition
6.3
. Using
g_{3}(K)=\tfrac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)-g-\kappa_{+}
, we rewrite the equations of
Proposition
6.3
(a) and (b).

\displaystyle V^{\mathrm{top}}_{md}(\widehat{K})

\displaystyle=-\frac{g}{2}-\frac{\eta_{+}}{4}+\min_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{+}+g}(R\left(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md+2j-2\kappa_{+}-2g\right)-

\displaystyle(md+j-\kappa_{+}-g))

\displaystyle V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{md}(\widehat{K})

\displaystyle=-\frac{g}{2}+\frac{\eta_{+}}{4}+\max_{0\leq i\leq g}\min_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}}

\displaystyle(R\left(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md+2j+2i-2g-2\kappa_{+}+\eta_{+}\right)-(md+i+j-g-\kappa_{+}+\eta_{+})).

Comparing this with (
6.7
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{+}+g}

\displaystyle R\left(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md+2j-2\kappa_{+}-2g\right)-

\displaystyle-(md+j-\kappa_{+}-g)\geq\tfrac{1}{8}(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1).

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq i\leq g}\min_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}}

\displaystyle R\left(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md+2i+2j-2(\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+})-\eta_{+}-2g\right)-

\displaystyle-(md+j-\kappa_{+}+\eta_{+}-2g)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{8}(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1)+g.

With a change
j\mapsto\kappa_{+}+g-j
in the first inequality and
i\mapsto g-i
,
j\mapsto\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}-j
in the second, we obtain.

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{+}+g}

\displaystyle R\left(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md-2j\right)-md+j\geq\tfrac{1}{8}(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1).

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq i\leq g}\min_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}}

\displaystyle R\left(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md-2i-2j-\eta_{+}\right)-md+j

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{8}(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1)+g.

With
m=k-\frac{d-3}{2}
, after straightforward calculations we obtain

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq j\leq g+\kappa_{+}}\left(R(kd+1-2j)+j\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2},

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq j\leq g}\min_{0\leq i\leq\kappa_{+}-\eta_{+}}\left(R(kd+1-\eta_{+}-2i-2j)+i+j\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g,

completing the proof.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

The next corollary specifies Theorem
6.4
to the case of a rational curve with no
T(2,2n)
singularities for
n>1
, that is, for a curve
with
m_{n}=0
for
n>1
. In this case,
\eta_{+}=m_{1}=\kappa_{+}
.

Corollary 6.8
.

Suppose a rational curve
C
of degree
d
has cuspidal singular points
z_{1},\dots,z_{\nu}
,
\kappa_{+}
ordinary double points
and no other singularities.
Then

(6.9)

\begin{split}R(kd+1-\kappa_{+})&\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}\\
\min_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{+}}\left(R(kd+1-2j)+j\right)&\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2},\end{split}

where
R
is the convolution of the semigroup counting functions of critical points
z_{1},\dots,z_{\nu}
.

On the other hand, specifying
m_{n}=0
for all
n
, but allowing arbitrary
g
recovers
the following result
of Bodnár, Borodzik, Celoria, Golla, Hedden and Livingston
[
1
,
4
]
:

Corollary 6.10
.

Suppose
C
is a cuspidal curve of genus
g
and degree
d
. Let
R
be the convolution of semigroup counting functions
of the singular points of
C
. Then

(6.11)

\begin{split}\max_{0\leq j\leq g}\left(R(kd+1-2j)+j\right)&\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g\\
\min_{0\leq j\leq g}\left(R(kd+1-2j)+j\right)&\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}.\end{split}

6.3.
Comparing (
6.9
) and (
6.11
)

We stop for a moment to compare the estimates in (
6.9
) and (
6.11
).
There is a general question: given a surface
\Sigma
in a closed four-dimensional
manifold
X
with
g(\Sigma)>0
, can we deform
\Sigma
to a surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
of smaller genus, at the cost
of introducing more singularities.
A special case of this question is the following. Suppose
C
is an algebraic curve in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
of genus
g
. Does there exist
an algebraic curve
C^{\prime}
with (topologically) the same singularities as
C
, except that it has one more double point and genus one smaller?

From our perspective, the difference between
C
and
C^{\prime}
is reflected by the different shape of inequalities (
6.9
)
and (
6.11
). To be more concrete, suppose
C
has genus
1
and let
R
be the convolution
of the semigroup counting functions for cuspidal singularities of
C
. The restrictions for
R
from (
6.11
)
are:

(6.12)

R(kd-1)\in\{K-1,K\},\ R(kd+1)\in\{K,K+1\},

where
k=1,\dots,d-2
and
K=\frac{1}{2}(k+1)(k+2)
. On the other hand, the restrictions for

R
from (
6.9
) (i.e. those for
C^{\prime}
) are:

(6.13)

R(kd)\leq K,\quad\min(R(kd+1),R(kd-1)+1)\geq K.

By using the fact that
R(i+1)-R(i)\in\{0,1\}
, the inequalities (
6.13
) imply:

(6.14)

R(kd-1)\in\{K-1,K\},\ R(kd+1)\in\{K,K+1\},\ R(kd)\leq K.

The inequality
R(kd)\leq K
does not follow from (
6.12
) and provides an obstruction
for trading genus to a double point.

Example 6.15
.

Equation (
6.11
) does not obstruct the existence of a degree
27
curve with genus
1
and a single singularity whose link is the
T(10,73)
torus knot.
Likewise, it does not obstruct the existence of a genus
1
curve of degree
33
with link of singularity
T(12,91)
; compare
[
4
, Theorem 9.1f and g]
.

However, using (
6.14
) we obstruct the existence of a rational curve with a single double point in the above cases: a degree
27
curve with
T(10,73)
-type singularity does not exist,
likewise a degree
33
curve with
T(12,91)
singularity does not exist.
In the first case the relevant value of
k
is
12
; in the second it is
7
.

We stress that we have no explicit constructions of the genus 1 curves in this example. We do not know if these genus 1 curves exist as complex curves, or even smooth curves of algebraic type.

Example 6.16

(Open Problem)
.

Let
\phi_{0}=0,\phi_{1}=1
,
\phi_{n}=\phi_{n-1}+\phi_{n-2}
be the Fibonacci sequence.
In
[
4
, Proposition 9.2]
, based on a construction of Orevkov
[
35
]
there was constructed a family of genus 1 cuspidal curves of degree
\phi_{4n}
with a
single singularity whose link is the
T(\phi_{4n-2},\phi_{4n+2})
torus knot (
n=2,3,\dots
)

Does there exist a genus zero curve
C_{n}\subset\mathbb{C}P^{2}
of degree
\phi_{4n}
with a singularity whose link is the
T(\phi_{4n-2},\phi_{4n+2})
torus knot and a single positive double point?

Remark 6.17
.

Theorem
6.4
does not obstruct the existence of a curve from Example
6.16
. An inductive argument involving Cremona transformation indicates that if
C_{n}
exists for a single
n
,
then it exists for all
n
; compare
[
35
, Section 6]
.

It is known that
C_{n}
cannot possibly exist in the algebraic category (refer to Subsection
1.5
). There are two different arguments.
First, for large
n
,
C_{n}
would violate the Bogomolov–Miyaoka–Yau
inequality (see
[
35
]
or
[
4
, Section 9.2]
for exemplary applications of the BMY inequality for
complex plane curves). Secondly, the existence of
C_{n}
implies, by passing to
C_{1}
, the existence of a rational cubic that is tangent to a given line with tangency order
8

(by an analog to the construction of
[
4
, Proposition 9.2]
). Explicit calculations of possible coefficients of such cubic lead to a contradiction.

The existence of
C_{n}
is related to the question on whether there exists an analog of a BMY inequality in the smooth category; see
[
25
]
.

6.4.
Negative double points

We now specify to the case where
C
is a surface which has a single algebraic singularity and
\underline{m}_{n}\geq 0
singular points whose links are negative

T(2,2n)
torus links.

Theorem 6.18
.

Suppose
C
is a genus
g
degree
d

singular curve in the smooth category as in Definition
1.3
with a cuspidal singular point
z
,

\underline{m}_{n}
singularities whose link is
-T(2,2n)
for each
n\geq 1
, and no other singular points. Suppose further that the
genus of
C
is given by (
6.1
).

Then, for any
k=1,\dots,d-2
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq j\leq g+\kappa_{-}}\left(R(kd+1-2j)+j\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g+\kappa_{-},

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq i\leq g}\max_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-}}\left(R(kd+1-2i-2j-\eta_{-})+i+j\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-},

where
R
is the semigroup counting function for the singular point
z
, and
\eta_{-}=\sum\underline{m}_{n},\kappa_{-}=\sum\underline{m}_{n}n
.

Proof.

The beginning of the proof is exactly the same as in the proof of Theorem
6.4
.
The boundary
Y
of the tubular neighborhood of
C
is a result of a surgery with coefficient
d^{2}

on the knot
\widehat{K}
in
\#^{2g+\eta_{-}}S^{2}\times S^{1}
. In particular, (
6.7
) holds
with
\eta_{-}
replacing
\eta_{+}
:

(6.19)

\begin{split}V^{\textrm{top}}_{md}(\widehat{K})&\geq\frac{(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1)}{8}-\frac{\eta_{-}}{4}-\frac{g}{2}\\
V^{\textrm{bot}}_{md}(\widehat{K})&\leq\frac{(d-2m+1)(d-2m-1)}{8}+\frac{\eta_{-}}{4}+\frac{g}{2}.\end{split}

With
g_{3}(K)=\tfrac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)-g-\kappa_{-}
,
equations of Proposition
6.3
(c) and (d) take the form:

\displaystyle V^{\mathrm{top}}_{md}(\widehat{K})

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{2}-\frac{\eta_{-}}{4}+\min_{0\leq i\leq g}\max_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-}}\big(R\big(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md-2j-2i-\eta_{-}\big)

\displaystyle-(md-i-j+g+\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-})\big)

\displaystyle V^{\mathrm{bot}}_{md}(\widehat{K})

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{2}+\frac{\eta_{-}}{4}+\max_{0\leq j\leq g+\kappa_{-}}\big(R\big(\tfrac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}+md-2j\big)

\displaystyle-(md-j+g+\kappa_{-})\big).

Comparing this with (
6.19
), after analogous changes as in Subsection
6.2
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq j\leq g+\kappa_{-}}\left(R(kd+1-2j)+j\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g+\kappa_{-},

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq i\leq g}\max_{0\leq j\leq\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-}}\left(R(kd+1-2i-2j-\eta_{-})+i+j\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+\kappa_{-}-\eta_{-},

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Corollary 6.20
.

Suppose
C
is a genus
g
degree
d
curve with a singular point
z
and
\eta_{-}
negative double points.
Assume that
C
has genus as in (
6.1
). Then, for
k=1,\dots,d-2
:

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq j\leq g+\eta_{-}}(R(kd+1-2j)+j)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g+\eta_{-}

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq j\leq g}(R(kd+1-\eta_{-}-j)+j)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
6.18
, noting that
\kappa_{-}=\eta_{-}
in our present situation.

/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

6.5.
Comparing Theorem
6.18
with Corollary
6.10

As in Subsection
6.3
we compare obstructions from Corollary
6.10

and Theorem
6.18
.
To this end, suppose
C
is a genus zero curve with a singular point
z
and a single negative double
point. Corollary
6.20
implies that for
k=1,\dots,d-2
we have

(6.21)

R(kd-1)+1,R(kd+1)\leq K+1,\ R(kd)\geq K,

where
K=\tfrac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}
.
The formula (
6.21
) bears a strong resemblance to (
6.13
). There is one difference.
In (
6.13
), that is, for curve with a positive double point, we require that
R(kd)\leq K
.
For curves with a negative double point, we have
R(kd)\geq K
. Note that the genus 1 case,
(
6.12
) admits values of
R(kd)
equal to
K-1,K,K+1
. If
R(kd)=K-1
, we cannot
trade the genus for a negative double point; if
R(kd)=K+1
, we cannot trade the genus for
a positive double point.

Example 6.22
.

The Orevkov curves of Example
6.16
are genus
1
curves, but
the genus cannot be traded for negative double points. A classical identity on Fibonacci numbers
\phi_{k-2}+\phi_{k+2}=3\phi_{k}

shows that the semigroup generated by
\phi_{4n-2}
and
\phi_{4n+2}
has precisely 9 elements in the interval
[0,3\phi_{4n})
.
These are
0,\phi_{4n-2},\dots,7\phi_{4n-2}
and
\phi_{4n+2}
. In particular
R(3\phi_{4n})=9<\frac{(3+1)(3+2)}{2}
, violating
the last condition of (
6.21
).

For example, for
n=2
, we have
d=21
and
R(s)
is the number
of elements less than
s
in the semigroup generated by
8
and
55
. For
k=3
we have
R(21\cdot 3)=9
, the relevant elements
in the semigroup being
[0,8,16,24,32,40,48,55,56]
.

7.
Algebraic curves with
A_{n}
singularities

Our next application deals with curves with
A_{n}
singularities.
Recall that the
A_{n}
singularity is a singularity whose link is a
T(2,n+1)
torus link/knot. The shift in the notation is due to the fact that the
A_{n}

singularity has Milnor number
n
.
A_{2k}
singularities are cuspidal, while
A_{2k+1}
singularities consist of two smooth branches with tangency. The family of curves with only
A_{n}
singularities encompasses all nodal curves (having only double points as singularities) and curves with nodes and ordinary cusps (having only double points
and positive trefoil knots as singularities). There is a significant research on such curves, see e.g.
[
16
,
17
,
30
]
. We refer to
[
15
]
for more general setting.
In the present section, we give bounds on the number of double
points and cusps from Heegaard Floer theory. These bounds are weaker than those obtained from
semicontinuity of the spectrum.

Throughout the section, we let
R_{n}
denote the
R
function of a
T(2,2n+1)
torus knot. More concretely

(7.1)

R_{n}(k)=\begin{cases}0&k\leq 0\\
\left\lfloor\frac{k+1}{2}\right\rfloor&k<2n+1\\
k-n&k\geq 2n+1.\end{cases}

Theorem 7.2
.

Suppose
C
is a curve with
a
ordinary double points,
b
cusps and degree
d
. Then

b\leq\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)-\frac{1}{2}A(d)-\frac{1}{2}a,

where

(7.3)

A(d)=\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}-6d+5}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is odd}\\
\frac{d^{2}-6d+4}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Observe that the
R
-function for the connected sum of
b
trefoils is
R=R_{b}
. This can be deduced as a consequence of Proposition
4.9
, or follows from more general statements of Bodnár, Feller, Krcatovich and Némethi
[
2
,
11
]
. The genus of
C
is equal to
g=\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)-a-b
. By Theorem
6.4
we obtain, for any
k=1,\dots,d-2
:

\displaystyle\max_{0\leq j\leq g}(R_{b}(kd+1-a-2j)+j)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq j\leq g+a}(R_{b}(kd+1-2j)+j)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}.

Consider the first inequality of the two (see Remark
7.5
below for the second inequality).
By letting
j=0
, the first inequality implies that

R_{b}(kd+1-a)\leq\frac{1}{2}(k+1)(k+2)+g

for all
k=1,\dots,d-2
. By the subsequent Proposition
7.6
, this is equivalent to

2g+a\geq A(d).

Combining this with the genus formula we obtain:

b\leq\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)-\frac{1}{2}A(d)-\frac{1}{2}a.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Note that for
a=0
, we have
b\leq\sim 0.375d^{2}
. Varchenko’s bound using semicontinuity of the spectrum gives

b\leq\sim\frac{23}{72}d^{2}
; the latter was reproved by the first author for singular curves in the smooth category as well, see

[
3
]
. The best bound that is known to the authors is due to Langer
[
28
]
. It says that

b\leq\sim\frac{125+\sqrt{73}}{432}d^{2}
. In
[
6
]
, there is constructed a family of curves with
b=\frac{2567}{8640}d^{2}

(for some infinite sequence of
d
).

Our next result concerns the maximal
n
for which there can exist a curve of degree
d
having an
A_{n}
singularity.
This is also a classical problem in the theory of plane curves.

Theorem 7.4
.

Suppose
C
is a degree
d
curve with an
A_{2n}
singularity and
h
double points. Then

n\leq\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)-\frac{1}{2}A(d)-\frac{h}{2}

where
A(d)
is the same function as the one in Theorem
7.2
.

Proof.

If
C
has other singularities than the
A_{2n}
singularity and double points, we can smooth them (in the smooth or algebraic category)
to obtain a curve with an
A_{2n}
singularity and double points. The genus of the curve is
g=\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)-n-h
. The
R

function for the
A_{2n}
singularity is
R_{n}
.
Acting exactly as in the proof of Theorem
7.2

we obtain the statement.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

The bound of Theorem
7.4
is assymptotically
2n\sim\frac{3}{4}d^{2}
, which is the same as the one
obtained from the signature function. Explicit constructions give families of curves with
2n\sim\frac{15}{28}d^{2}

[
17
]
,
2n\sim\frac{7}{12}d^{2}

[
36
]
and
2n\sim(4-2\sqrt{3})d^{2}

[
7
]
.

Remark 7.5
.

In this section, we have been using only the first inequality in (
6.5
). We will
now argue that the second inequality is trivially satisfied for curves with only
A_{2n}
singularities and cusps.
Suppose curve
C
has genus
g
,
h
double points and the
R
function is
R_{m}
for some
m
.
The second inequality of (
6.5
), after some calculations
reads:

R_{m+g+h}(kd+1)\geq\frac{1}{2}(k+1)(k+2).

Note that
m+g+h=\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)
, so we eventually obtain
R_{(d-1)(d-2)/2}\geq(k+1)(k+2)/2
. The inequality is independent on
m,g,h
, which is immediate to verify and trivially satisfied by any
non-singular complex curve with genus
g
and degree
d
.

In the rest of this section, we prove Proposition
7.6
, which was a technical step in the proof of Theorems
7.2
and
7.4
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Suppose
d\geq 4
and
\eta,g\geq 0
. Set

(7.7)

m=\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}-\eta-g,

and suppose further that
m>0
. Then

(7.8)

R_{m}(kd+1-\eta)\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g

for all
k\in\{1,\dots,d-2\}
if and only if

(7.9)

2g+\eta\geq\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}-6d+5}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is odd}\\
\frac{d^{2}-6d+4}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is even}.\end{cases}

We first prove that if
kd+1-\eta
is outside of the range
0,\dots,2m+1
, then (
7.8
) is always satisfied:

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose
m
is as in the statement of Proposition
7.6
and
1\leq k\leq d-2
. If
kd+1-\eta\geq 2m+1
or
kd+1-\eta\leq 0
, then

R_{m}(kd+1-\eta)\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g.

Proof.

If
kd+1-\eta\leq 0
, then
R_{m}(kd+1-\eta)=0
, so the claim is clearly true.

If
kd+1-\eta\geq 2m+1
, then by (
7.1
),
R_{m}(kd+1-\eta)=kd+1-\eta-m
. Using the value of
m
in (
7.7
) and rearranging, we obtain

\begin{split}&\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g-R_{m}(kd+1-\eta)\\
=&\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}-kd-1+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{2}\\
=&\frac{(d-k-1)(d-k-2)}{2}.\end{split}

For
1\leq k\leq d-2
, the above expression is nonnegative, concluding the proof.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare

Proof of Proposition
7.6
:

Suppose first that
k
is an integer which satisfies
0<kd+1-\eta<2m+1
. In this case, equation (
7.8
) is equivalent to the inequality

(7.11)

\left\lfloor\frac{kd+2-\eta}{2}\right\rfloor\leq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+g.

We note that, for any integers
x
and
y
, the inequality
\lfloor x/2\rfloor\leq y
is equivalent to the inequality
x\leq 2y+1
. Hence, (
7.11
) is equivalent to the inequality

kd+2-\eta\leq(k+1)(k+2)+2g+1.

Multiplying out and rearranging, this is equivalent to the expression

(7.12)

-k^{2}+(d-3)k-1\leq 2g+\eta.

For real
k
, the left-hand side is maximized at
(d-3)/2
. When
d
is odd,
(d-3)/2
is an integer and the maximum value of the left-hand side over integral
k
is

\frac{d^{2}-6d+5}{4},

If
d
is even, then the left-hand side of (
7.12
) attains its maximum value over the integers at both
k=(d-2)/2
and
k=(d-4)/2
. The value is

\frac{d^{2}-6d+4}{4}.

Note that
(d-2)/2
and
(d-4)/2
are
\lceil(d-3)/2\rceil
and
\lfloor(d-3)/2\rfloor
, respectively.

Note that if
kd+1-\eta<0
, or
kd+1-\eta>2m+1
, then (
7.8
) automatically holds by Lemma
7.10
. Since
R_{m}(s)=\lfloor(s+1)/2\rfloor
for
s\in\{0,\dots,2m+1\}
, we see that a sufficient condition for (
7.8
) is that

(7.13)

2g+\eta\geq\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}-6d+5}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is odd}\\
\frac{d^{2}-6d+4}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is even}.\end{cases}

This is also clearly necessary if

0\leq kd+1-\eta\leq 2m+1,

for either
k=\lfloor(d-3)/2\rfloor
or
k=\lceil(d-3)/2\rceil
(note that both of these values of
k
are in the range
1,\dots,d-2
, unless
d=4
, in which case just
\lceil(d-3)/2\rceil
is).

On the other hand, if

(7.14)

\lceil(d-3)/2\rceil d+1-\eta\geq 2m+1,

we claim that (
7.13
) holds automatically, and hence is vacuously a necessary condition for (
7.8
). To see this, write
\lceil(d-3)/2\rceil=(d-3+\delta)/2
where
\delta
is
0
if
d
is odd and
1
if
d
is even. Then (
7.14
) expands to the inequality

(7.15)

\left(\frac{d-3+\delta}{2}\right)d+1-\eta\geq(d-1)(d-2)-2\eta-2g+1.

We rearrange equation (
7.15
) to see that it is equivalent to

2g+\eta\geq\frac{d^{2}-(\delta+3)d+4}{2}.

However, it is easy to check

\frac{d^{2}-(\delta+3)d+4}{2}\geq\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}-6d+5}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is odd}\\
\frac{d^{2}-6d+4}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is even}.\end{cases}

Additionally, we consider the case that

(7.16)

\lfloor(d-3)/2\rfloor d+1-\eta\leq 0.

Write
\lfloor(d-3)/2\rfloor=(d-3-\delta)/2
, where
\delta
is either 0 or 1.
We expand out equation (
7.16
) to see that it is equivalent to

\eta\geq\frac{d^{2}-(3+\delta)d+2}{2}.

The left-hand side is less than or equal to
\eta+2g
. On the other hand, one easily computes

\frac{d^{2}-(3+\delta)d+2}{2}\geq\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}-6d+5}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is odd}\\
\frac{d^{2}-6d+4}{4}&\text{ if $d$ is even},\end{cases}

when
d\geq 4
, completing the proof.

user /Subtype /Link
/A ¡¡ /S/SetOCGState /State [/Toggle ] ¿¿
/Border [0 0 0]

\blacksquare
